School: Elm Tree Elementary School 3 of 260 F DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 4 of 260 Section 1: Overview of Comprehensive Education Planning Section 1: Overview of Comprehensive Education Planning Overview of Comprehensive Education Planning Comprehensive Education Planning (CEP) is a structured, data-driven process that guides schools in assessing their needs, identifying areas for growth, setting strategic goals and implementing targeted actions to improve student outcomes. Collaboratively developed by the School Leadership Team (SLT) in accordance with the requirements of Chancellor’s Regulation A-655, the CEP meets New York State Education Department (NYSED) requirements under the Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA) and aligns with NYC Public Schools’ (NYCPS) system-wide priorities. By engaging all stakeholders—school leaders, teachers, families, students and community members—schools can create a data-informed, equity- focused roadmap for student success and continuous improvement. Schools should focus their CEP on the five system-wide priorities below, connecting their planning to the data discussions and conversations with stakeholders held as part of the school’s needs assessment: Priority 1: All students learn to read well Priority 2: All students are physically and emotionally safe Priority 3: All students have a high-quality academic experience Priority 4: All students graduate college and career ready and have a strong plan and pathway to economic security Priority 5: All districts and schools are more inclusive and responsive for parents and families, including having more families choose NYC Public Schools The Education Planning Process The CEP follows an equity-driven, continuous improvement cycle, rooted in the system-wide priorities. This begins with a comprehensive needs assessment of the school’s most current data to identify priority needs and root causes. Next, the SLT establishes specific, measurable, attainable, relevant, and timely— SMART goal(s) aligned to each priority and determines targets to measure progress toward meeting the annual goals. The SLT and instructional leaders define key evidence-based key strategies, professional learning activities and parent, family and community empowerment efforts. They monitor the implementation of those strategies and activities and track progress through measurable targets. Then, the SLT aligns the school-based budget with human and instructional resources to support action plans. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 5 of 260 As schools set goals, develop key strategies, and plan action steps, they provide opportunities for all children to meet NYSED’s proficient and advanced levels of student academic achievement. These include evidence based instructional strategies that address the educational needs of historically underserved populations and tackle structural inequities. Schools indicate how the plan addresses the needs of Multilingual/English Language Learners (ML/ELL) through development of the school’s Language Allocation Policy (LAP) & Title III Application (if applicable) and the Language Translation and Interpretation (LTI) Plan, that are required as part of the CEP. Schools also address the needs of Students with Disabilities (SWD) through the High Quality Inclusive & Accessible Learning for Students with Disabilities section of the plan. SLTs also develop a plan to improve attendance and reduce chronic absenteeism. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 6 of 260 Guidance on Conducting a Comprehensive Needs Assessment Directions: Conduct a comprehensive needs assessment for each Priority. The information below provides guidance for reviewing and analyzing student and school performance trends to uncover root causes for low student achievement. 1. Review Multiple Data Sources: Use the most current and available information including, but not limited to, the following sources of formative and summative data, as applicable: Qualitative and quantitative data, including enrollment and class size trends 2024-25 ESSA Accountability Data Instructional Leadership Framework (ILF), Supportive Environment Framework (SEF) Inventories, and NYC School Survey results Quality Review Reports, NYSED and NYCPS CSI or A/TSI Support Visits, Receivership Quarterly Reports, and Diagnostic Tool for School and District Effectiveness Framework (DTSDE) NYC School Quality Guide, Insight, New Visions, NWEA MAP and/or other formative assessment data, IEP Review Survey Data, Progress on IEP Goals Data, Program (SESIS-STARS Linkage) Reports, Mandated (Related) Services Reports, and NYCPS Tools for Understanding Your Data for student subgroup information 2. Analyze Data Trend(s) to Identify Areas of Strength and Need: Identify data trends by grade, cohort, and content area(s) to surface gaps and inequities. Compare data sets to identify instructional and school practices that are impacting student performance. Guiding questions to consider: What does the data reveal about improving and declining student performance trends within grades, cohorts, and student subgroups? What inequities are evident between highest and lowest performing student subgroups? Which subgroup(s) scored at Level 1 based on NYSED ESSA accountability indicators? What does the data suggest regarding the effectiveness (or ineffectiveness) of current school programs, practices, and/or strategies/initiatives? What areas may need to be strengthened? Are there any differences in student performance between program delivery models for ML/ELL and SWD? (e.g., for special education: special class, ICT, SETSS, related services only; for ML/ELL: Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) vs. Dual Language, etc.) How does delivery of instruction factor into data trends? What are the implications for teacher quality and professional learning? What inequities in special education referral and school disciplinary practices (e.g. suspensions, removals) are evident between student subgroups with IEPs? 3. Identify Root Cause(s): After analyzing the data, engage in a process for uncovering root causes or contributing factors for low student and school performance. Develop theories or hypotheses to explain DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 7 of 260 why these needs exist and why your school is getting its current outcomes. Root causes could be related to gaps and inequities in areas such as curriculum & instruction, school systems & structures, student support services, school climate & safety, and family & community engagement. Focus on the causes, rather than the symptoms. Use the 5 Whys Method or other methods for identifying root causes. Consider the questions below: What theories or hypotheses explain the current student and school outcomes? Are there root causes specific to certain subgroups, grades, cohorts, or content area(s)? Additional resources and supports are available below. Use this CEP Needs Assessment Worksheet to help your SLT and stakeholders identify appropriate data sources, data trends, root causes and priority needs. For guidance and best practices on engaging stakeholders in a comprehensive needs assessment and root cause analysis, see this deck. A current list of all CEP/Title I resources can be found on the iPlan Portal Public Resources Page. Under the Federal Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA), NYS Education Law and Commissioner’s Regulations, stakeholder participation in the development of the CEP must include multiple constituencies who represent the school community. This summary and the entire Comprehensive Education Plan (CEP) should be shared with your school community. Please note that this summary and the CEP will be made available to the public through the iPlan Portal, an online space where school stakeholders can go to collaborate in the development of the school’s education plans. Provide your school’s mission statement: Elm Tree Elementary School is a community that extends past the physical building. We are a safe, nurturing, respectful learning community where students are encouraged to purposefully explore and innovate through technology, both independently and cooperatively. Our students are unique individuals with diverse learning styles who are socially and emotionally responsive. They are challenged with rigorous curricula and supported as they become our future leaders of tomorrow. Our students are problem solvers, collaborators, and inventors. They think critically and ask questions as they develop a love for learning that will enable them to adapt to change and persevere through challenges. As the 21st Century continues to evolve, we are committed to enhancing, improving, engaging and stimulating a learning environment that is accessible for all students. Through the dedication of our school staff, the support of our parents, and the partnerships we create within our community, our students will leave us as culturally and linguistically diverse individuals that are prepared for their future. Elm Tree Elementary School believes that planning for success in the future means working together today. Written by the Elm Tree School Community Develop a narrative summary that includes contextual information about your school’s community and its unique/important characteristics and partnerships: Include demographic information about your school’s student population. Elm Tree Elementary serves a community of Pre-Kindergarten through Fifth Grade with a total of 888 students, 59% of the student population has a ENL Classification. Of our ENL students 94% are primarily Spanish Speaking. Our programs are inclusive of Monolingual, Dual Language, Transitional Bilingual, Small Class, Monolingual STEM ICT and Dual Language ICT. Our student make up is inclusive of 7% Asian, 5% Black, 88% Hispanic, 2% White, 18% SWD, 47% Male and 53% Female. By ethnicity, the ELLs at our school are made up of 5.9% Non-Hispanic ELLs and 76.8% Hispanic ELLs. Of the Non-Hispanic ELLs, 11 are Asian, representing 32.4% of ELLs at our school, 10 are black (29.4%), and 11 are white (32.4%). Of the Hispanic ELLs, 370 are American Indian or Alaskan Native, representing 76.8% of ELLs at our school, 7 are black (1.5%), 18 are multiracial (3.7%), 5 are Native Hawaiian or Other Pacific Islander (1.0%), and 82 are White (17.0%). Our school aims to develop and strengthen its partnerships with the local community. For the 2025-2026 school year, we will continue a Partnership with the Child Center of New York, which is a Community Based Organization DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 16 of 260 that will support our school community and its needs. Since over 88% of our population is Spanish Speaking and approximately 2% are other languages such as Chinese, Nepali, Bengali, Fulani, and Arabic, we are sure to be inclusive of all language translations in all that we do. We are a Responsive Classroom School, which means that we view the entire child and incorporate social emotional learning into each content area that is taught. Briefly summarize the key initiatives your school will implement this school year. This school year, our initiatives are focused on targeted interventions and professional development to enhance student achievement. We will implement tier 2 and tier 3 small group instruction in ELA and Math to address diverse learning needs. Utilizing mClass as a screening tool, we will identify and address foundational gaps, with ongoing progress monitoring for tier 2 and tier 3 students. Our grade-wide inquiry will revisit progress monitoring data within each grade level, facilitating collaborative discussions among teacher teams to identify common areas of success and areas needing improvement in student learning outcomes. Professional growth opportunities include HMH professional learning sessions led by HMH Consultant, complemented by weekly professional development series targeting K-2 and 3-5 educators. These initiatives aim to foster a supportive and responsive learning environment that ensures every student receives the necessary support to succeed academically. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 25 of 260 Priority 1 Action Plan Directions: Identify the key strategy and progressive action steps your school will take to address the specific priority needs and root causes identified for this goal(s). More than one strategy can be indicated if multiple and/or differentiated needs for this priority have been identified. Key strategies should be key levers for success and may represent something that is new to the school. Action steps to operationalize key strategies should focus on new activities or initiatives and/or modifications to current initiatives (e.g., expanded to reach a wider audience or refined for greater impact on students). Note: Routine activities your school does every year should not be included. Key Strategy 1 (Required): Implement a comprehensive, school-wide literacy plan grounded in the Science of Reading that prioritizes data- driven instruction, targeted small group interventions, and vertically aligned practices across Grades K–5. This strategy will be supported by consistent progress monitoring using mClass data, strategic deployment of MTSS supports, and ongoing professional development to ensure all students receive instruction tailored to their specific literacy needs—accelerating growth and moving more students to benchmark and above by June 2026. Root Cause Addressed: We're still in development of norming and strengthening approaches in the Science of Reading. Strengthening this will continue to support students progress. Additionally, small group instruction and intervention have not always been strategically planned or aligned to student needs. This strategy addresses the need for coherence, consistency, and responsive instruction across K–5 to ensure all students receive the supports necessary to meet or exceed benchmark expectations. Action Steps: Indicate what your school will do specifically to implement each strategy to achieve this annual goal. Include both student-facing activities and professional learning for staff. Be sure to indicate how action steps will be introduced and rolled out. Action Steps Strengthen Core Literacy Instruction Across K–5: All classrooms will implement Science of Reading-aligned instructional strategies with fidelity. Instruction will include structured phonics, phonemic awareness, vocabulary, and comprehension components. Literacy block expectations will be monitored through regular classroom walkthroughs, intervisitations, and collaborative planning to ensure alignment and consistency across all grades. Implement Consistent Small Group Instruction: Teachers will use districted aligned assessments and progress DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 26 of 260 monitoring data to form flexible small groups tailored to students’ specific skill gaps. Small group instruction will occur throughout the literacy block using targeted materials and skill-based lesson plans. This ensures differentiated support for students at all performance levels. Provide Ongoing Professional Learning and Coaching: Professional development will be provided to deepen teacher understanding of the Science of Reading and enhance instructional practice. Use Inquiry Team Meetings: Weekly meetings will be held to analyze district aligned assessment data and determine next steps for instruction and intervention. Teachers will develop action plans using subskill data (e.g., NWF, ORF) and track student movement across performance levels. This cycle will help ensure timely instructional adjustments based on student progress. Promote Vertical Alignment and Collaboration: Grade-band PLCs/ Intervisitation Initiatives will focus on aligning instructional practices and expectations across K–5. Teachers will examine student work, share strategies, and review learning progressions to ensure skills build cohesively from one grade to the next. This collaboration will support smoother transitions and consistent literacy growth across the school. Key Strategy 2 (Optional): Root Cause Addressed: Action Steps: Indicate what your school will do specifically to implement each strategy to achieve this annual goal. Include both student-facing activities and professional learning for staff. Be sure to indicate how action steps will be introduced and rolled out. Action Steps Implementation Monitoring: Indicate your primary structures to progress monitor implementation of key strategies and action steps. Include specific data/evidence you will collect and use to determine progress and impact on instruction, student learning, and achievement. Note: Implementation Progress Checks will be conducted in the Progress Reporting Tool (PRT) in the iPlan Portal for the 2025-26 school year. To monitor the implementation of our literacy improvement strategies, ILT will establish clear structures and routines that ensure accountability, track progress, and measure impact on instruction and student outcomes. -Primary Structures for Monitoring: Instructional Walkthroughs and Observation Cycles: Admin and instructional DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 27 of 260 coaches will conduct regular classroom walkthroughs using the District's ELA Implementation Tool to monitor implementation of the Science of Reading, small group instruction, and use of district aligned assessment data. -Weekly Inquiry Team Meetings: Grade-level teams will participate in structured Inquiry Team meetings to review district aligned assessment data, progress monitoring results, and student movement across performance bands. -Coaching Logs and Feedback Loops: ILT will document teacher support, modeling, and feedback related to core instruction, small group practices, and intervention planning. -Tiered Support Trackers: MTSS tracking tools will be used to monitor student participation in Tier 2 and Tier 3 interventions, along with short-cycle assessment data to evaluate impact. Start Date End Date Strategies to Support Family & Community Engagement Partners/CBOs Leveraged 09/04/2025 06/25/2026 To support the successful implementation of our literacy Child Center of improvement plan, we will engage families, including both birth New York and foster parents, through targeted, inclusive strategies that promote partnership, communication, and shared responsibility for student learning. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 29 of 260 Priority 1 Budget and Resources Budget and Resources Directions: Indicate available and anticipated resources that will be leveraged to achieve this annual goal and implement this action plan, including human resources, instructional resources (e.g., curricula, programs), schedule adjustments, other. Use N/A if not applicable and/or indicate other resource needs. Human Resources To implement our literacy improvement plan, we will leverage MTSS Providers and interventionists to support Tier 2 and Tier 3 students, as well as instructional coaches to provide ongoing professional learning and feedback to teachers. Classroom teachers will lead Science of Reading-aligned instruction and small group lessons. Additionally, our attendance team staff and parent coordinator will support outreach and organize literacy-focused events. Instructional Resources We will utilize Science of Reading-aligned curriculum materials and mClass (DIBELS 8th Edition) as our core tools for universal screening, progress monitoring, and instructional planning. Additional resources include decodable readers, small group intervention materials, and planning templates. Family engagement will be supported with translated reading resources and take-home strategies that align with classroom instruction. Schedule Adjustments Protected literacy blocks (Chippy ELA) will be maintained across all K–5 classrooms to ensure uninterrupted time for core instruction and small group work. Teachers will also be provided with regular planning time to review mClass data and adjust instruction accordingly. Data meetings will be built into the schedule, and time will be set aside for literacy workshops and family engagement events beyond the standard school day. Action Steps Implementation Monitoring: Indicate your primary structures to progress monitor implementation of key strategies and action steps. Include specific data/evidence you will collect and use to determine progress and impact on instruction, student learning, and achievement. Note: Implementation Progress Checks will be conducted in the Progress Reporting Tool (PRT) in the iPlan Portal for the 2025-26 school year. To monitor the implementation of key strategies and action steps, we will establish structures for progress monitoring. First, regular classroom observations will be conducted by administration, SEL Team and instructional coaches to assess whether Responsive Classroom practices are being effectively implemented. These observations will be paired with teacher self-assessments and feedback sessions to identify areas of strength and opportunities for growth. We will also track teacher participation in professional development, ensuring that all educators complete required DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 41 of 260 training and attend follow-up coaching sessions. Data from post-training surveys and feedback will be collected to measure the effectiveness of PD and identify any emerging needs. Additionally, we will use student surveys conducted twice per year to gather direct feedback on how safe and supported students feel in their classrooms. This data, alongside school climate surveys, will be analyzed to gauge the impact of Responsive Classroom practices on student engagement, behavior, and overall well-being. Finally, student achievement data (such as academic performance, attendance rates, and behavioral incident reports) will be regularly reviewed to evaluate whether the implementation of Responsive Classroom practices is having a positive impact on student learning and achievement. This comprehensive monitoring approach will allow us to adjust the strategy as needed, ensuring that both teaching practices and student outcomes continue to improve over time. To ensure effective implementation of our key strategy, we will use a combination of observational data, incident trends analysis, and stakeholder feedback to monitor progress and adjust supports in real time. Primary Monitoring Structures: Crisis and Safety Team Meetings: Monthly review of behavior data, student and teacher feedback, and implementation checkpoints to assess progress and make informed decisions. Classroom Walkthroughs: Conducted by administrators, SEL Team and coaches using a look-for tool aligned to Responsive Classroom and Tier 1 SEL practices (e.g., Morning Meeting routines, behavior norms, proactive redirection). Behavior Incident Data: Monthly OORS and Alert Form reviews of trends (e.g., location, behavior type, repeat incidents) to assess the impact of behavior supports and identify priority areas. Restorative Practices Tracking: Monitoring use of reflection sheets, follow-up conversations, and class community meetings to ensure restorative systems are being used consistently. Student Climate Surveys: Fall and spring school-based surveys to measure changes in students' sense of emotional safety, belonging, and support from adults. Key Strategy 1 (Required): Strengthen and vertically align Tier 1 math instruction in Grades K–5, with a focus on developmentally appropriate practice in Grades 1–2 and integration of language supports. We are refining our key strategies based on walkthrough feedback from our district math coach and Generation Ready. Classroom visits showed that while students were engaging with math tasks, there were limited opportunities for structured math discourse. As a result, we adjusted our action steps to more explicitly prioritize student talk: teachers are now planning specific discourse moves (turn-and-talks, sentence stems, math vocabulary prompts, and share-outs) before, during, and after lessons so that students regularly explain their reasoning and use precise mathematical language. Root Cause Addressed: This plan addresses several key root causes impacting math achievement in Grades K–5. Regular vertical planning strengthens alignment and coherence of math instruction across grade levels, directly addressing inconsistencies in curriculum and teaching practices. Implementing flexible, data-driven intervention groups in Grades 1 and 2 ensures timely support for students with foundational skill gaps. Embedding language supports and scaffolds into math instruction helps teachers meet the needs of linguistically diverse learners and improves access to academic vocabulary. Finally, ongoing monitoring through walkthroughs, data analysis, and collaborative inquiry meetings promotes developmentally appropriate instruction in early grades and continuous refinement of practices, ensuring all students are supported in mastering math content and language. Be sure to indicate how action steps will be introduced and rolled out. Action Steps Instructional Alignment & Professional Learning: Use professional learning time to unpack priority standards in early DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 53 of 260 grades and vertically align math skills and progressions across K–5. Facilitate regular vertical planning sessions for K– 5 teachers to share best practices and ensure instructional coherence. Targeted Intervention Systems: Implement flexible intervention groups in Grades 1 and 2 based on i-Ready diagnostics and MAP Growth data to close foundational gaps early. Monitor student progress frequently and adjust interventions as needed using multiple data sources. Language Supports and Scaffolds: Embed language development strategies and academic vocabulary instruction within math lessons and intervention sessions to meet diverse learner needs. Provide teachers with resources and coaching on integrating language scaffolds effectively. Ongoing Monitoring and Feedback: Conduct monthly walkthroughs and observations focused on foundational math instruction and language supports, providing actionable feedback. Use student work samples, observation data, and assessment trends to inform coaching and professional development. Maintain weekly inquiry team meetings to analyze data and collaboratively refine instruction and support. Explicitly prioritizing student talk: teachers will receive professional learning on discourse moves (turn-and-talks, sentence stems, math vocabulary prompts, and share-outs) before, during, and after lessons so that students regularly explain their reasoning and use precise mathematical language. Root Cause Addressed: Students have limited structured opportunities to engage in authentic civic experiences (public speaking, advocacy, decision-making about shared resources). Financial literacy is not consistently embedded in 4th- and 5th-grade curriculum, leading to gaps in students’ understanding of budgeting, saving, and planning. Teachers need clear programs, resources, and dedicated time to implement Civics for All and financial literacy with fidelity. Families and students may be unaware of how civics and financial literacy connect to long-term economic security and future planning. To monitor implementation of our Civics for All and financial literacy work, we will use monthly Civics for All District meetings, grade team meetings, and check-ins with our Civics for All point person and Bethpage Federal Bank. Classroom walkthroughs and observations in 4th and 5th grade will be used to look for evidence of civics instruction, student discourse, and financial literacy learning. Key Strategy 1 (Required): Establish and implement school-wide Family Engagement Guidelines that are co-created with families to ensure they are purposeful, aligned to priority needs, and designed to be accessible, inclusive, and welcoming. By actively involving families in the development of these guidelines, the school will give families a voice in the process, ensuring their needs and perspectives are reflected. Root Cause Addressed: The implementation of Family Engagement Guidelines directly addresses several key root causes of inconsistent participation. By establishing a clear and consistent process for planning events, we ensure that all family engagement opportunities are intentionally aligned to school priorities and student learning outcomes, addressing the gap in perceived relevance. Multi-channel outreach strategies and flexible scheduling options respond to barriers such as timing conflicts and inconsistent communication. Additionally, incorporating welcoming elements like snacks, raffles, and child-friendly components helps foster a more inclusive and inviting environment, encouraging broader and more sustained family participation. Action Steps: Indicate what your school will do specifically to implement each strategy to achieve this annual goal. Include both student-facing activities and professional learning for staff. Be sure to indicate how action steps will be introduced and rolled out. Action Steps Family Input for Co-Creating Family Engagement Guidelines: Send out a survey to all families to gather input on the types of events they find most valuable, accessible, and aligned with their needs. The survey will also be shared and discussed at the first PTA meeting, giving families an additional opportunity to provide feedback in person. The input DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 80 of 260 collected from both the survey and the meeting will help co-create the Family Engagement Guidelines and ensure that future events are inclusive, purposeful, and strengthen the school-family partnership. Draft and Share Family Engagement Guidelines: Create a clear, staff-facing guide outlining expectations for aligning events to school priorities, ensuring accessibility, and incorporating inclusive practices. Distribute a Standardized Planning Template: Give staff a simple form to complete for all family events, including event purpose, connection to school priorities, outreach plan, and supports provided (e.g., childcare, meals, translation). Establish an Event Approval & Support Process: Create a system where proposed events are reviewed by a designated team (e.g., Admin, ILT or PTA Teacher Team) to ensure alignment with guidelines and provide support as needed. Ensure Inclusive Communication & Promotion: Use multiple platforms (Edlio, text, flyers, website, social media, teacher outreach) to advertise events in families’ home languages and ensure broad awareness. Track Attendance and Gather Feedback; Collect sign-in sheets and brief post-event surveys to monitor participation and understand family needs and preferences. Celebrate and Share Successes: Highlight successful events in staff newsletters or grade team meetings to recognize strong practices and encourage continued engagement. To monitor the implementation of our Family Engagement Guidelines and associated action steps, we will use participation/attendance data to assess progress and impact. The primary structures for monitoring will include: -Parent Coordinator and PTA Teacher Team Reviews: These teams will meet monthly to review event proposals, attendance data, and staff submissions using the standardized planning template. They will provide feedback and ensure events align with school priorities. -Participation Tracking: We will collect and analyze sign-in sheets to monitor increases in family attendance across events over time, aiming for a 25% increase by June 2026. -Family Feedback Surveys: Short, post-event surveys will be distributed to families in their home languages to capture their experiences, satisfaction, and suggestions for improvement. This will inform adjustments to event format and content. -Midyear and End-of-Year Data Reviews: Parent Coordinator and PTA Teacher Team Reviews will conduct two formal reviews of all collected data to assess whether family engagement efforts are leading to stronger partnerships, more informed families, and an increased sense of connection to student learning. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 93 of 260 DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 94 of 260 Attendance/Chronic Absenteeism Action Plan Directions: Identify the key strategy and progressive action steps your school will take to address the specific priority needs and root causes identified for this goal(s). More than one strategy can be indicated if multiple and/or differentiated needs for this priority have been identified. Key strategies should be key levers for success and may represent something that is new to the school. Action steps to operationalize key strategies should focus on new activities or initiatives and/or modifications to current initiatives (e.g., expanded to reach a wider audience or refined for greater impact on students). Note: Routine activities your school does every year should not be included. Key Strategy 1 (Required): Implement a Targeted Attendance and Family Engagement Initiative that combines proactive interventions, early- grade attendance support, and consistent family partnerships to reduce chronic absenteeism and increase overall student attendance. Proactive intervention is a key strategy that enables the school to intervene early before attendance issues become long-term patterns. By regularly monitoring attendance data and setting clear thresholds (e.g., students approaching 10% absences), staff can flag students who are at risk and begin supportive outreach. This early action allows the school to engage families, identify underlying barriers, and implement targeted interventions such as check-ins, mentoring, or schedule adjustments. Root Cause Addressed: This strategy addresses key factors contributing to attendance challenges, including seasonal dips during winter months, inconsistent early interventions for at-risk students, and barriers faced by families such as healthcare, transportation, and childcare needs. Chronic absenteeism is particularly high in early grades where strong attendance habits are not yet established, and communication with families has not always been timely or consistent. By targeting these root causes, the strategy aims to build early attendance routines, strengthen outreach, and provide timely, family-centered support. Action Steps: Indicate what your school will do specifically to implement each strategy to achieve this annual goal. Include both student-facing activities and professional learning for staff. Be sure to indicate how action steps will be introduced and rolled out. Action Steps Family Attendance Workshops and Early-Grade Support: Beginning in October, the Attendance Team, ILT, PTA, and DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 95 of 260 parent volunteers will co-host monthly workshops for all families, with a targeted focus on building strong attendance habits in Pre-K–2. Sessions will feature interactive discussions, at-home attendance strategies, and opportunities for families to co-create solutions with staff. Families will receive translated morning routine toolkits, ongoing follow-up support from the Parent Coordinator and teachers, and recognition during each session for improved attendance. Implement Real-Time Attendance Monitoring and Rapid Response: Attendance data from ATS, Insight and New Visions will be reviewed to flag students with 5+ absences. Administration will meet bi-weekly to identify attendance trends within their grade levels and come up with action plans to share with Attendance team members monthly. Staff will be supported in immediately contacting families through calls, texts, or home visits, and each at-risk student will be paired with an Attendance Mentor for weekly check-ins. Attendance Incentives: We will continue implementing a variety of initiatives with a strong focus on addressing winter attendance challenges. Classrooms demonstrating the greatest improvement will be recognized through family celebration events and school-wide acknowledgments. Monthly newsletters and Elm Tree App updates will provide attendance tips and highlight student and class success stories to encourage consistent attendance. Provide Health and Family Resource Supports: We will collaborate with school-based health providers and CBOs to connect families with medical, childcare, and transportation resources. Family resource fairs hosted by our CBO will help address barriers that frequently lead to absences. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 96 of 260 Note: Implementation Progress Checks will be conducted in the Progress Reporting Tool (PRT) in the iPlan Portal for the 2025-26 school year. To ensure consistent progress toward reducing chronic absenteeism and improving overall student attendance, Elm Tree Elementary will implement a structured, data-driven monitoring system aligned to our key strategies and action steps. Primary Monitoring Structures: -Attendance Team Meetings: Monthly meetings with the Attendance Team to review ATS, Insight and New Visions attendance data, identify at-risk students, and monitor intervention follow-through. -Family Engagement Reviews: Workshops and attendance events will include feedback forms and attendance logs to track family participation and emerging needs. -Biweekly Administration Attendance Reviews: School leaders will analyze attendance trends, chronic absenteeism rates, and progress toward goals to adjust strategies as needed. Administration will support teachers in outreach efforts. Data and Evidence Collected: -Daily and Weekly Attendance Reports: Ongoing monitoring of overall attendance percentages and identification of students with 5+ absences. Chronic Absenteeism Tracking: Monthly reports from ATS and New Visions showing the percentage of students classified as chronically absent (goal: reduction from 20% to 15%). Family Engagement Data: Attendance at workshops, resource fairs, and conferences, along with translated survey responses measuring family understanding of attendance expectations. Intervention Documentation: Logs of attendance mentor check-ins, home visits, and parent meetings, reviewed monthly by attendance team for follow-up actions. Incentive Participation: Records of student and class recognition events and improvements during seasonal attendance campaigns. Impact on Instruction and Student Learning: -Improved attendance will result in increased instructional time for students, particularly in early grades where chronic absenteeism is highest. -Progress will be measured by the correlation between improved attendance and growth in academic benchmarks, including MAP Growth math and reading assessments. -Attendance data will be reviewed alongside formative assessments and classroom observation data to ensure students benefiting from increased instructional time demonstrate measurable gains in learning and engagement. Human Resources: Special Education Teachers, IEP Teacher Instructional Resources Core Curriculum and Intervention Programs that are systematic Schedule Adjustments N/A Other Resources Needed N/A DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 114 of 260 Section 6: Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) Section 6: Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) Academic Support NYCPS Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) align with NYSED guidelines for providing Academic Intervention Services (AIS) to students who have not yet met state standards or are considered to be at- risk of not meeting state standards. The Multi-tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) framework, organizes supports into three tiers: Tier 1 provides schoolwide, high-quality programming for all students, including SWDs; Tier 2 offers data informed small-group interventions for at-promise students; and Tier 3 delivers intensive, individualized support for students with persistent needs. This facilitates the strategic planning and necessary resource alignment to ensure their school community appropriately and adequately serves all students well, in partnership with students and families. MTSS identifies the academic support services to be provided and ensures that students receive Response to Intervention (RtI) and Positive Behavioral Interventions and Support (PBIS) strategies as needed. See NYSED’s memo to view the criteria for determining AIS services. The Supportive Environment Framework (SEF) offers a wide range of evidence-based Tier 2 and Tier 3 interventions and supports for student social-emotional, behavioral, physical and mental wellness, with particular attention to the unique needs of students in Temporary Housing and/or Foster Care, who may face additional challenges related to school stability, trauma and access to educational resources. A culturally responsive-sustaining approach to intervention embeds culturally relevant texts and historical narratives into literacy and social studies interventions, ensuring students receive the support that reflects their identities and lived experiences, increasing engagement and achievement. Use the charts below and a Multi-Tiered System of Support (MTSS) to match academic, social/emotional, behavioral and instructional resources with student needs to provide targeted support for all learners, especially at-risk students who experience difficulty meeting state standards, including those living in Temporary Housing and/or residing in Foster Care placements. Indicate specialized materials, technology- based tools and/or extra instruction, including Expanded Learning Time that supplement school-wide systems & supports and/or classroom level supports. Ensure that interventions address the unique social- emotional, behavioral and academic challenges faced by culturally and linguistically diverse students, as well as those impacted by housing instability and child welfare system involvement. Our school shares the essential components of MTSS with families in multiple ways to ensure transparency and collaboration. During the fall and spring family workshops, we explain how MTSS supports academic, behavioral, and social-emotional development through tiered interventions. Families are informed of the MTSS process during parent-teacher conferences, IEP/504 meetings, and Student Support Team (SST) discussions. Communication is provided in families’ home languages and includes explanations of tiered supports, progress monitoring tools, and how decisions are made using data. We also use newsletters, our school website, and family-facing flyers to share updates about MTSS programming and strategies they can use at home. When students receive Tier 2 or Tier 3 interventions, families are contacted directly and invited to participate in developing support plans. Describe how families receive updates on the progress of their child(ren) who receive Tier 2 targeted, or Tier 3 intensive interventions. Families of students receiving Tier 2 or Tier 3 interventions receive weekly updates on their child’s progress. Teachers and interventionists communicate through phone calls, emails, or home-school communication logs, depending on the family’s preferred method. These updates include information on the student’s intervention goals, type of support, frequency, and progress based on weekly monitoring data. All communication is delivered in the family’s preferred language to ensure clarity, collaboration, and consistency in supporting the child’s growth Describe how families are involved during decision making regarding the progress of students receiving Tier 3 intensive intervention. Families of students receiving Tier 3 intensive interventions are actively involved in decision-making through scheduled check-ins with teachers, interventionists, and support staff. These conversations occur in the family’s preferred language and include updates on progress, review of intervention strategies, and discussion of any necessary adjustments. Families are invited to share insights about their child’s learning and behavior at home, ask questions, and collaborate on next steps. This shared decision-making process ensures that interventions remain responsive to each student’s individual needs. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 119 of 260 Students in Temporary Housing (STH) Support As included in your school’s Office of School and Youth Development Consolidated Plan-STH Section and in accordance with the federal McKinney-Vento Homeless Assistance Act and Chancellor's Regulation A- 780, schools must identify, serve, and report on students living in temporary housing (STH). All Title I and non-Title I schools receive a Title I allocation for STH students. The Students in Temporary Housing (STH) website provides guidance on STH pupils and how these funds can be used to meet their needs, as well as restrictions. For more information on acceptable uses of the mandated Title I, Part A set-aside to support your STH population, please refer to the Title I STH Funds Toolkit. Identify the number of Students in Temporary Housing who are currently attending your school and provide a breakdown of their temporary housing status. Since the number of STH children can change regularly, schools must carefully track this number to determine whether there has been an increase in need. Number of students in temporary housing who are: Doubled up (with friends or relatives because they cannot find or afford housing): Living in a shelter or transitional shelter: Living in a hotel/motel: Other *Note: In keeping with established practices regarding personally identifiable information, use the letter “s” to identify a number less than five (5). At Elm Tree Elementary, 390 students are identified as living in temporary housing, according to the New Visions Portal (August 2025). After a careful analysis of historical demographic, attendance and achievement data for your STH population, what trends do you notice, and how can these trends inform the services and support provided for this population? After a careful analysis of historical demographic, attendance, and achievement data for our students in temporary housing (STH), several key trends have emerged that inform our approach to services and supports. Of our 390 students identified as STH, 94 (24%) are chronically absent, pointing to barriers such as unreliable transportation, housing instability, and health-related challenges. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 120 of 260 Academically, only 19% of STH students are meeting or exceeding grade-level expectations in reading, as measured by our most recent i-Ready ELA data, compared to 38% of the general student population. In math, just 17% of STH students are on grade level, compared to 37% of peers. These data points confirm the need for intensified academic intervention and wraparound supports. Additionally, 274 STH students (70%) are classified as English Language Learners, and 65 students (17%) have Individualized Education Programs (IEPs), indicating a compounded need for differentiated instruction, language scaffolds, and coordinated special education services. Findings from the annual Title I STH Parent Survey underscore challenges related to access to hygiene products, food security, and mental health services, which correlate with engagement and achievement. These results are used to prioritize services, including providing MetroCards and travel support, expanded access to mental health professionals, after-school tutoring, and small group academic interventions during the day. By using this data, the school is refining its MTSS model to ensure responsive interventions for the STH population, while working closely with families and community partners to support whole-child needs that directly impact learning outcomes. Describe the services you are planning to provide to the STH population. How were those services selected to best meet students’ needs? How will you (or did you) utilize the STH Title I Survey to select services aligned to need? To effectively support our students experiencing homelessness (STH), we are implementing services tailored to their unique challenges and needs. We will provide reliable transportation assistance to combat chronic absenteeism, which is a significant issue due to the lack of stable transportation options. Academic tutoring and support, including before/after-school programs and access to technology, will help bridge the achievement gap observed among STH students. We are also expanding social-emotional learning (SEL) programs to address the emotional and psychological stressors associated with homelessness, including counseling and resilience-building workshops. To ensure STH students have the necessities required for a stable learning environment, we will supply basic resources such as food, clothing, hygiene products, and school supplies. Language support services will be offered to the many STH students who are English Language Learners, while those with Individualized Education Programs (IEP) will receive enhanced special education services. Additionally, we are engaging families through workshops and support services to help them navigate educational and social resources, fostering a supportive home environment. Health and wellness services will be provided in partnership with local health organizations to address physical and mental health needs. These services were selected based on a thorough analysis of demographic, attendance, and achievement data, and insights from the STH Title I Survey, ensuring that they directly address the most pressing needs of our STH population. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 123 of 260 Describe how your school will meet the requirement of ensuring that ELT instruction is delivered under the supervision of a teacher who is NYS certified in that content area. To meet the requirement that ELT (Extended Learning Time) instruction is delivered under the supervision of a teacher who is New York State (NYS) certified in the relevant content area, our school ensures that all ELT sessions are taught by our experienced teaching staff, who hold the necessary NYS certifications. Each ELT session is meticulously planned and led by these certified educators, ensuring that instruction adheres to state educational standards and leverages their specialized knowledge in core academic subjects. Our certified teachers are responsible for the careful integration of core curricula, such as HMH for ELA and Envision for Math, into ELT programming, supplemented by additional materials designed to meet the diverse needs of all learners. This alignment ensures that the instruction provided during ELT is of the same high quality and rigor as that offered during the regular school day. Furthermore, our teachers collaborate to design and implement curriculum enhancements that are coherent with the daytime academic program during before and after school grade wide curriculum mapping. This collaboration guarantees that all ELT instruction is not only aligned with but also supervised by educators with the appropriate certifications, thereby meeting and exceeding the state’s requirements for educational standards. Through this comprehensive approach, we provide a cohesive, robust educational experience that supports and enriches student learning while ensuring compliance with NYS certification requirements for all instructional staff. Describe how your school will meet the requirement of ensuring the alignment of ELT instruction with core academic subject area instruction offered during the regular school day. To ensure the alignment of ELT (Extended Learning Time) instruction with the core academic subjects offered during the regular school day, Elm Tree Elementary will leverage the expertise of our New York State (NYS) certified teaching staff. Each ELT session will be meticulously planned and led by educators who are certified in the relevant content areas, guaranteeing that instruction not only adheres to state educational standards but also aligns seamlessly with the daytime curriculum. Our certified teachers will utilize core curricula, such as HMH for ELA and Envision for Math, which are already in use during the regular school day. By employing these established resources, supplemented with additional materials tailored to meet the diverse needs of all learners, we ensure consistency in instructional goals and learning outcomes across both regular and extended learning times. Moreover, our teachers will collaborate to design and implement curriculum enhancements that are coherent with the daytime academic program. This collaboration allows for the integration of the same teaching methodologies, learning objectives, and assessment strategies used during regular school hours. For example, before-school programs will include targeted mCLASS Tier 2 and Tier 3 small group sessions that directly address areas needing improvement, while after-school programs will offer specialized support such as IEP Student Help, Math Games, Reading Centers, and more, each aligned with the core academic subjects. During the Chippy ELA Experiences Block, our students will engage in activities such as Heggerty, Sound Sensible, SPIRE, and iReady, which complement the instruction they receive during regular class time. This alignment ensures that students benefit from a cohesive and robust educational experience, reinforcing their learning and addressing individual needs in a consistent and effective manner. By maintaining this alignment, our ELT sessions support the overall academic growth of our students, enhancing their learning and optimizing their educational outcomes. The School-Parent Compact (SPC) is a component of the Parent and Family Engagement Policy (PFEP) that outlines how parents, the entire school staff, and students will share this responsibility. The activities and/or strategies included in your school's Parent and Family Engagement Policy should align with current Comprehensive Education Plan (CEP) goals for improving student achievement and should be developed or updated with input and feedback from parent leaders and the Parent Coordinator. In addition, if the school community will be engaged in central parent engagement initiatives, such as Parent Academy, which will provide training for school communities to help strengthen family-school partnerships, please be sure to include these activities in the school's policy. In Title I schools, the Title I Parent Advisory Council (PAC) represents the Title I parent community in supporting parent and family engagement. The Title I PAC Chairperson will serve in a consultative role with the SLT representing the school’s Title I parents. Schools are encouraged to use the sample Parent and Family Engagement and SPC templates below (which meet federal Title I parent and family engagement requirements) as guidance for updating the school's current policy. Parent and Family Engagement Policy Elm Tree Elementary School Parent and Family Engagement Policy At Elm Tree Elementary, we believe that strong family-school partnerships are essential to student success. In alignment with Section 1116 of Title I, Part A of the Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA), our Parent and Family Engagement Policy is rooted in our commitment to ensuring every family is informed, empowered, and engaged in their child’s academic journey. Policy Objectives and Practices: Shared Responsibility for Student Success: Elm Tree actively involves families in the shared responsibility for DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 126 of 260 student learning through a collaboratively developed School-Parent Compact (SPC). The compact outlines how families, school staff, and students will work together to support academic achievement. The SPC is reviewed annually with parent input during Title I Parent Advisory Council meetings, SLT sessions, and parent workshops. Annual Title I Meeting and Ongoing Communication: We host an annual Title I meeting to inform families of our school’s Title I program, parents’ rights, and the services available to eligible students. In addition, regular communication occurs through monthly newsletters, our school app, Edlio website updates, and multilingual documents to ensure all families receive timely updates. Collaborative Planning with Parent Leadership: Parent voice shapes our engagement strategies. The Parent Coordinator works closely with the Title I PAC Chairperson, the SLT, and the PTA to co-plan monthly workshops, family events, and informational sessions that align with our CEP goals, including improving attendance, early literacy, and multilingual learner achievement. Parent feedback is regularly gathered through surveys, suggestion boxes, and after-event evaluations. Parent Learning Opportunities: In support of our goal to build parent capacity, Elm Tree hosts a robust calendar of family workshops, literacy and math workshops, and digital access training. Topics are chosen in collaboration with parent leaders and are designed to strengthen families’ ability to support learning at home and engage with school resources. Welcoming and Inclusive Environment: All families are welcome at Elm Tree. Our multilingual staff, ENL team, and family outreach team ensure communication is accessible. Family engagement events are culturally responsive, with translation services and materials available in our predominant home languages (Spanish, Bengali, and others as needed). Continuous Improvement: The Parent and Family Engagement Policy is reviewed and revised annually in partnership with the Title I PAC, PTA, SLT, and Parent Coordinator. We assess effectiveness through attendance at events, family survey data, and ongoing feedback to ensure our policy reflects the evolving needs of our school community. Elm Tree Elementary is committed to cultivating a culture of collaboration, trust, and high expectations in which families are true partners in learning. Parent/Guardian Responsibilities At Elm Tree Elementary, we believe families are a child’s first and most important teachers. As partners in learning, parents and guardians play a vital role in supporting their child’s success both in and out of the classroom. To help every child reach their highest potential, parents and caregivers agree to: Promote regular and punctual attendance by ensuring their child arrives to school on time and following school procedures when the child is absent. Establish healthy routines at home, including setting an appropriate bedtime so their child arrives at school well- rested and ready to learn. Support learning at home by checking homework daily, offering help when needed, and reading with or discussing reading with their child for at least 15 minutes each day. Limit screen time and encourage their child to engage in enriching after-school activities like extended learning, clubs, sports, or quality family time. Discuss school expectations and this Compact with their child, encouraging them to follow school rules and contribute to a positive learning environment. Volunteer when possible, either in person at school or from home, and participate in school activities and events. Communicate regularly with their child’s teacher, staying informed about academic progress and addressing educational needs. This includes reading and responding to school and district notices in a timely manner. Provide feedback through surveys or other requests and take part in decision-making around their child’s education. Engage in workshops or trainings offered by the school, district, or state to better understand teaching and learning practices. Participate in school-based leadership and advisory groups, such as the Parent Association, PTA, Title I Parent Advisory Council, or School Leadership Team. Embrace shared responsibility for their child’s academic growth and overall success. Together, through mutual respect and shared commitment, we will ensure that every child at Elm Tree thrives academically, socially, and emotionally. III. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 135 of 260 Title I Program Information Title I Program Information Part 1: Title I Program Type Title I Requirements and Strengthening Title I Parent Involvement: Under USDOE’s Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA), Title I schools are required to address professional learning and parent and family engagement in the Comprehensive Education Plan (CEP). To strengthen parent leadership in Title I schools, and increase the involvement of all parents of Title I eligible children in improving students’ academic outcomes, all Title I schools are required to establish a Title I Parent Advisory Council (PAC) that will serve as the consultative and representative body for all Title I parents in the school to provide voice into the spending of Title I funds. The SLT is responsible for facilitating consultation with the Title I Parent Advisory Council regarding the joint development of the CEP, Parent and Family Engagement Policy, School Parent Compact (SPC) and the use of the 1% parent involvement set-aside in Title I schools. The Title I Parent Advisory Council (PAC) Chairperson (or alternate) is expected to attend all regular meetings of the School Leadership Team (SLT). Title I parents, as represented by the Title I PAC Chairperson, must be consulted regarding the use of the Title I 1% set-aside for parent and family engagement. The Title I 1% set-aside funding is used to supplement parent and family engagement activities as described in the school’s Parent and Family Engagement Policy and must be aligned with student achievement goals as outlined in the CEP. For additional guidance on the Title I Parent Advisory Council (PAC), go to the Protocols and Timeline for Title I Parent and Family Engagement Activities document. Your school’s Title I Program Title I SWP DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 136 of 260 Part 2: All Title I Schools High Quality and Ongoing Professional Learning: Please list below the Priority or section(s) that indicate strategies and activities for high quality professional learning for teachers, principals, staff, and paraprofessionals to enable all students to meet state standards. At Elm Tree Elementary, we are committed to providing high-quality, ongoing professional learning for all staff, including teachers, paraprofessionals, school aides, school leaders, and all other personnel. The CEP priorities guide our work in building staff capacity to improve student outcomes, foster inclusive classroom practices, and cultivate a safe, supportive learning environment. We begin by conducting regular staff surveys and needs assessments to identify the specific professional learning needs across instructional staff and support personnel. These insights inform the development of differentiated professional learning pathways that address the unique roles and responsibilities of educators, including targeted sessions for co-teaching teams, paraprofessionals, and early-career teachers. In alignment with our CEP priorities, we offer professional learning opportunities that include: -Responsive Classroom and SEL training to support implementation of Tier 1 behavior systems and promote a positive, inclusive school culture (Priority 2) -Standards-aligned planning and instructional strategies to strengthen core academic instruction and deepen content knowledge (Priorities 1, 3, 4, 5) -Data inquiry cycles and collaborative planning protocols to build educator capacity in using student data for instructional decision-making (Priorities 1 and 3) Culturally and linguistically responsive practices that address the diverse needs of our multilingual learners and students with IEPs (Priorities 1, 3, 4, 5) To ensure coherence and transparency, we consistently align all professional learning with the goals and benchmarks outlined in our CEP. These goals are reviewed during ILT and teacher team meetings, reinforcing a shared understanding of expectations and progress. Additionally, staff receive regular feedback and coaching, grounded in observation data and aligned to the Danielson Framework, to support implementation of new practices. Ongoing reflection and progress monitoring are built into our cycles of professional learning, fostering a culture of continuous improvement. Finally, all training is data-driven and responsive to student needs. We use data from i-Ready, MAP Growth, behavior reports, and family surveys to inform both the content and timing of PD sessions, ensuring learning experiences are timely, relevant, and impactful. Through these coordinated strategies, Elm Tree Elementary is creating a professional learning ecosystem that directly supports our school’s CEP priorities and advances equity, academic achievement, and student well-being. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 137 of 260 Part 4: Schoolwide Program (SWP) Transition Plans to Assist Preschool Children (Elementary Schools Only): Describe the transition plans used to assist preschool children from early childhood programs to the elementary school program (e.g. aligned curriculum, joint PD & parent involvement activities, sharing of records/information, early intervention services, etc.). At Elm Tree Elementary, we have developed comprehensive transition plans to assist preschool children in their transition from early childhood programs to our elementary school program. These plans are designed to provide a smooth and supportive experience for both the children and their families, ensuring that the young learners are well- prepared for the new educational environment. 1. Aligned Curriculum: To create continuity between preschool and elementary education, we work closely with early childhood programs to align our curriculum with theirs. This alignment includes integrating foundational skills in literacy, numeracy, and social-emotional development that are critical for kindergarten readiness. By ensuring that the curriculum flows seamlessly from preschool to elementary school, we help children build on their existing knowledge and skills, making the transition smoother and more effective. 2. Joint Professional Development (PD) & Parent Involvement Activities: We organize joint professional development sessions for preschool and elementary school teachers. These sessions focus on best practices in early childhood education, effective transition strategies, and aligning instructional approaches. By fostering collaboration between preschool and elementary educators, we ensure that both sets of teachers are equipped to support the children’s transition. Additionally, we engage parents through workshops and orientation sessions that provide information on what to expect in kindergarten and how they can support their child’s learning at home. These activities help parents become active participants in their child’s educational journey, strengthening the home-school connection and easing the transition. 3. Sharing of Records and Information: We maintain an open line of communication with early childhood programs to facilitate the sharing of student records and important information. This includes assessments, developmental milestones, and any special needs or accommodations. By having access to comprehensive records, our kindergarten teachers can better understand each child’s strengths and areas for growth, enabling them to tailor their instruction to meet individual needs right from the start. 4. Early Intervention Services: For children who require additional support, we collaborate with early childhood programs to provide early intervention services. This may include speech and language therapy, occupational therapy, or behavioral support, which continues as they transition to elementary school. By identifying and addressing these needs early, we can ensure that all children have the support they need to thrive in the new school setting. 5. Transition Activities and Visits: DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 138 of 260 We offer a variety of transition activities, including school tours, classroom visits, and meet-and-greet events with teachers and future classmates. These activities help familiarize children with the new environment and the people they will interact with, reducing anxiety and building excitement for the next phase of their education. 6. Individualized Transition Plans: For children who may need additional support during the transition, we develop individualized transition plans. These plans outline specific steps and strategies to help the child adjust to the new setting. This may involve gradual integration into the classroom, additional meetings with the new teacher, or personalized support from school counselors or special education staff. 7. Ongoing Support and Monitoring: Once the children have transitioned to kindergarten, we continue to monitor their progress closely. Teachers and support staff work together to ensure that any challenges are addressed promptly, and additional support is provided as needed. Regular check-ins with parents help keep them informed and involved in their child’s progress, ensuring that the transition remains positive and effective. Through these comprehensive and collaborative efforts, Elm Tree Elementary is committed to providing a supportive and seamless transition for preschool children, helping them to build a strong foundation for future academic success. Explanation/Background: Title I Schoolwide Program schools are expected to use the flexibility available to them to integrate services and programs with the aim of enhancing the entire educational program and helping all students reach proficient and advanced levels of achievement. In addition to coordinating and integrating services, Schoolwide Program schools may combine most Federal, State and local funds to provide those services. By consolidating funds from Federal, State, and local sources, a Schoolwide Program school can address its needs using all its available resources. This gives a school more flexibility in how it uses available resources to meet the identified needs of all its students. Consolidating funds in a Schoolwide Program means that a school treats the funds it is consolidating like they are a single “pool” of funds. In other words, the funds from the contributing programs in the school lose their individual identity and the school has one flexible pool of funds. The school uses funds from this consolidated Schoolwide pool to support any activity of the Schoolwide Program without regard to which program contributed the specific funds used for a particular activity – so long as the use of funds meets the intent and purposes of the programs from which the funds originate. To consolidate funding in a Schoolwide Program, the school does not literally need to combine funds in a single account or pool with its own accounting code. Rather, the word “pool” is used conceptually to convey that a Schoolwide Program school has the use of all consolidated funds available to it for the dedicated function of operating a Schoolwide Program without regard to the identity of those funds. Consolidating Federal funds in a Schoolwide Program has the following additional advantages: Consolidating Federal funds eases the requirements for accounting for funds from each specific program separately, because a Schoolwide school is not required to distinguish among funds received from different sources when accounting for their use. A school that consolidates Federal funds in its Schoolwide Program is not required to meet most of the statutory and regulatory requirements of the specific Federal programs included in the consolidation (e.g., semi-annual time and effort reporting for Title I). However, the school must ensure that it meets the intent and purposes of the Federal programs included in the consolidation so that the needs of the intended beneficiaries are met. 2. The intent and purposes of the Federal programs indicated on the chart above (Part 4c of this section) are as follows: Title I, Part A – Schoolwide Programs: To upgrade the entire educational program in the school in order to improve the academic achievement of all students, particularly the lowest-achieving students. This includes provision of services for Students in Temporary Housing (STH). Title I 1003(a), School Improvement: Comprehensive Support and Improvement (CSI), Additional Targeted Support and Improvement (ATSI) and Targeted Support and Improvement (TSI) Funding: DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 141 of 260 Support implementation of school improvement plans that aims to improve instruction and address the identified needs Title II, Part A: Supplementary funding to improve student academic achievement by reducing class size in grades K, 1, 2, and 3, with an emphasis on grades with an average register greater than 20. If space is not available to form additional classes, funds may support push-in teacher(s) to supplement the instructional program. For schools that choose to reduce class size, priority must be given to reducing class size in general education settings and must demonstrate the following: Reduce class sizes to 15-18 students or fewer (including the use of co-teaching and floating teachers that bring the student to teacher ratio to one teacher per 15-18 students); Be accompanied by a rigorous curriculum, effective teachers, and a sustained, job-embedded professional development plan. Title III, Part A: To help ensure that children with limited English proficiency become proficient in English, develop high academic attainment in English, and meet the same challenging State academic content and achievement standards in the core academic subjects that all other children are expected to meet. Another purpose of this program is to increase the capacity of schools to establish, implement and sustain high-quality language instruction programs and English language development programs that assist schools in effectively teaching students with limited English proficiency. Title III, Part A is also designed to promote the participation of parents and communities of limited English proficient children in English language instruction programs. Title III Immigrant: Supplementary and enhanced services to immigrant students, in the areas of English language acquisition and content area achievement. Immigrant children and youth are defined as individuals who are aged 3 through 21, have not been attending school in any one or more states for more than three (3) full academic years, and were not born in any state. “State” means the 50 states of the United States, the District of Columbia, and the Commonwealth of Puerto Rico. Children born to U.S. citizens abroad, the U.S. Virgin Islands, or any other U.S. territory that is not D.C. or Puerto Rico are to be considered immigrants. Title IV, Part A: Title IV funds are for schools that are among those with the greatest needs, have the highest numbers of students from low-income families, are identified for CS, ATSI or TSI, or are identified as persistently dangerous public schools. Allowable uses of funds under well-rounded education, safety and health, and educational technology include direct services for students, professional development for teachers and administrators, and supplemental educational resources. School allocations have been provided for well-rounded education, social emotional learning, cultural responsiveness awareness, professional development to support implementation of technology and blended learning environments, and community coordinators in schools with high rates of students in temporary housing Students in Temporary Housing (STH): Schools must describe their use of Title I STH funds in their consolidated application.
