DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 159 of 260 Describe your English as a New Language (ENL), and if applicable Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE), and Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) programs. For each program, include: How stand-alone ENL is implemented as per CR Part 154 How integrated ENL is implemented as per CR Part 154 How students are grouped (e.g., block [class travels together as a group], ungraded [all students regardless of grade are in one class], heterogeneous [mixed proficiency levels], homogenous [proficiency level is the same in one class]). a. English as a New Language (ENL) Stand-alone ENL: In accordance with CR Part 154, Stand-Alone English as a New Language (ENL) instruction is provided to English Language Learners (ELLs) and Multilingual Learners (MLs), with instructional time determined by each student’s English proficiency level. Students at the Entering (Beginning) level receive one unit of study (180 minutes per week), while Emerging level students receive half a unit (90 minutes per week). During Stand-Alone instruction, students are grouped by language proficiency level and work with ENL teachers who focus on developing foundational skills such as vocabulary acquisition and conceptual understanding. This model is often paired with co-teaching or integrated ENL instruction, particularly for students at the beginning stages of English language development. Stand-Alone ENL classes are structured around students’ language and literacy levels, using a developmental framework to provide targeted instruction. Language arts and content-area instruction are delivered in English, supported by research-based strategies such as the use of visuals, response frames, fluency building, and unified- class discussion expectations. Instruction may also incorporate students’ home or primary languages to support comprehension and facilitate learning. While students are typically grouped by grade level within Stand-Alone classes, flexible grouping across two consecutive grades may be used when appropriate and feasible. The overarching goal of this instructional model is to support students in reaching the state-designated level of English proficiency for their grade level and to ensure they meet the New York State learning standards. Integrated ENL: English Language Learners (ELLs) and Multilingual Learners (MLs) receive both core content area instruction and English language development through Integrated ENL instruction. This model incorporates the use of students’ home languages as a support, along with targeted instructional strategies to enhance comprehension and engagement. Integrated ENL classes are either taught by a dually certified teacher (ENL and content area) or co-taught by a certified ENL teacher and a certified content area teacher. Students are grouped by language proficiency level and receive instruction based on the following time allocations: Entering: 180 minutes of Integrated ENL/ELA Emerging: 180 minutes of Integrated ENL/ELA, plus 90 minutes of Integrated ENL in a core content area (flexible) Transitioning: 90 minutes of Integrated ENL/ELA, plus 90 minutes of Integrated ENL in a core content area (flexible) Expanding: 180 minutes of Integrated ENL in ELA or another core content area Commanding: 90 minutes of Integrated ENL in ELA or a core content area DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 160 of 260 The level of support and instructional strategies used are differentiated based on students' language proficiency levels. Students at the Entering Level, who make up 10.7% of our ELL population, are at the very beginning stages of English language development. These students require the highest level of language support. Instruction for these students emphasizes basic vocabulary acquisition, survival language, and the development of early listening and speaking skills. In Integrated ENL settings, ENL teachers provide highly scaffolded instruction using visuals, gestures, realia, modeling, and first language support when possible. Co-teaching models, inclusive of parrallel teaching and alternative teaching maybe used so that Entering students can receive focused small-group instruction while still participating in the general education setting. These students are often grouped together during targeted instructional periods to build foundational English skills in a supportive environment, while also being given opportunities to engage with more proficient peers for language modeling and social integration. Students at the Emerging level, who make up 21.8% of our ELL population, are at the beginning stages of English language development. In Integrated ENL settings, these students receive intensive scaffolding, including the use of visuals, modeling, sentence frames, and home language support when appropriate. Co-teaching models such as parallel teaching are often used to allow ENL teachers to work with small groups, reinforcing foundational vocabulary and language structures necessary for content access. For students at the Transitioning level, who represent 23.2% of our ELLs, instruction emphasizes the development of academic vocabulary, reading comprehension, and the ability to participate in structured academic conversations. These students are beginning to use English more independently, so lessons often include collaborative work and opportunities for speaking and writing across content areas. ENL and content-area teachers co-deliver instruction, ensuring that language goals are integrated into content learning. Our Expanding students, who comprise 34.3% of our ELL population, are approaching full proficiency in English and are capable of engaging in more complex academic tasks. ENL support at this level focuses on refining oral and written language, increasing the complexity of vocabulary and sentence structure, and strengthening comprehension in academic contexts. Instruction is more seamlessly embedded into the general classroom experience, with the ENL teacher supporting whole-group instruction and using formative assessment to identify remaining language needs. Our Former ELL Students, who comprise 10.0% of our population, still receive continued support integrated in ELA/Core Content Area Instruction within the classroom. ENL Support at this level focuses on key strategies including explicit teaching of academic vocabulary, scaffolded instruction (e.g., sentence stems, visuals, graphic organizers), and ongoing progress monitoring. Supports are targeted yet rigorous, helping students develop academic language while maintaining high expectations, with attention to their cultural and linguistic assets. These supports typically continue for up to two years post-exit. ELLs/MLs are placed in heterogeneous classrooms and primarily receive Integrated ENL instruction during their literacy periods. Instruction is scaffolded to support the development of speaking, listening, reading, and writing skills. Students are often divided into two strategic learning groups, with teachers using parallel teaching methods to deliver the same content through differentiated strategies tailored to students’ language needs. However, we also employ co-teaching models to support explicit modeling and instruction of content material with an emphasis on the language demands of the tasks. Teachers are expected to establish clear instructional goals that support both language development and content mastery. Integrated ENL instruction is designed to seamlessly blend language acquisition with academic content, using scaffolding techniques to make lessons accessible while providing meaningful opportunities for language practice. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 161 of 260 Instruction includes explicit teaching of language structures and features relevant to the content area, such as academic vocabulary, language functions, sentence structures, and discourse patterns. Teachers also incorporate culturally responsive content and practices, fostering inclusive environments that value students’ linguistic and cultural backgrounds. Formative assessment is an integral part of instruction. Teachers use ongoing observation, questioning, and informal assessments to monitor students’ language development and content understanding. Timely, constructive feedback is provided to guide students’ progress. Collaborative learning is also emphasized. ELLs engage in group work, cooperative learning, and peer tutoring to construct meaning, negotiate understanding, and develop language proficiency in authentic, interactive contexts. By implementing these strategies, Integrated ENL instruction supports the simultaneous development of language and academic content knowledge, empowering ELLs to succeed in mainstream classrooms and meet New York State learning standards. b. Bilingual Education (If applicable) Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE): Elm Tree Elementary School offers a Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) program for students in Grades 3 through 5. This program is designed to support English Language Learners (ELLs) in developing English proficiency while continuing to access grade-level academic content in their home language, Spanish. Instruction in Spanish serves as a bridge, allowing students to build a strong academic foundation as they transition to English. As students’ English language skills improve, the amount of instruction in Spanish is gradually reduced, with the ultimate goal of transitioning students into an English-only instructional setting. The TBE program at Elm Tree Elementary is grounded in the belief that students can achieve academic success in both languages. It aims to help students master grade-level content in Spanish while developing the English language skills necessary to thrive in mainstream classrooms. Once students reach the required level of English proficiency, they transition out of the bilingual setting and continue their education in English, while still being encouraged to maintain and develop their multilingual and multiliterate abilities. The program also emphasizes the importance of cultural identity, encouraging students to take pride in their heritage while fostering an appreciation for the diverse cultures and perspectives within the school community. Instruction in the TBE program includes core content areas taught in both English and Spanish. As students' progress in their English language development, more instructional time is dedicated to English, while support in the home language is gradually reduced. Scaffolding is provided across all language domains—speaking, listening, reading, and writing—to ensure students can access and engage with the curriculum. Both English as a New Language (ENL) and Home Language Arts (HLA) instruction are delivered to meet mandated instructional minutes and support students’ academic and linguistic growth. Classrooms in the TBE program consist of students who share Spanish as their home language but may have varying levels of English proficiency, however we primarily have students who at the Beginning Stages of Language Development programmed in this model type. Instruction is differentiated to meet the diverse needs of learners, and teachers use a range of strategies to support language development and content mastery. The program is designed not only to facilitate a smooth transition to English-only instruction but also to affirm students’ cultural and linguistic identities, ensuring they feel valued and supported throughout their educational journey. Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE): DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 162 of 260 Elm Tree Elementary School offers a Dual Language program using the 50/50 Stand-Alone Dual Language model of instruction where students alternate the language of instruction each day. The only grade that is currently still employing a side by side model is Grade 4. This program is designed to foster bilingualism and biliteracy by delivering instruction in both English and Spanish. In the 50/50 model, students receive equal instructional time in each language, typically divided evenly across the week, allowing for balanced language development. Instruction in the Dual Language program is carefully structured to support growth in all four language domains: listening, speaking, reading, and writing. All classroom instruction is delivered in both English and Spanish, with ENL (English as a New Language) and HLA (Home Language Arts) services provided to meet mandated instructional minutes—360 minutes for Entering and Emerging students, and 180 minutes for Transitioning and Expanding students. Teachers employ a range of instructional strategies, including immersion, translanguaging, and code- switching, to enhance comprehension and facilitate learning in both languages. Instructional materials, resources, and assessments are available in both English and Spanish, ensuring that students can access grade-level content and make meaningful connections across languages. Students in the Dual Language program engage daily with peers who speak both English and Spanish, creating rich opportunities for authentic language practice and social interaction. Collaborative learning experiences such as group projects, peer tutoring, and cooperative activities not only support language development but also promote cross-cultural understanding and appreciation. Classrooms are composed of students with varying levels of language proficiency, creating a heterogeneous learning environment that encourages peer support and differentiated instruction. This inclusive model ensures that all students, regardless of their proficiency level, are supported in developing strong academic and linguistic skills in both languages. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 163 of 260 2. How does your school ensure the mandated number of instructional minutes are provided according to students’ English language proficiency levels in each program model? Elm Tree Elementary School ensures that all English Language Learners (ELLs) and Multilingual Learners (MLs) receive their mandated instructional minutes in both English and the target language. Through our Dual Language program, the majority of students in Grades K–5 meet their required instructional minutes based on their English proficiency levels. English Language Arts (ELA) is delivered on designated English days, while Home Language Arts (HLA) is provided on Spanish days, maintaining a balanced approach to bilingual instruction. Students in our monolingual Special Education 12:1:1 classrooms receive their mandated instructional minutes through an integrated model. In this setting, a TESOL-certified teacher collaborates closely with the special education teacher to support language acquisition and vocabulary development. Instruction is delivered in the core content areas and includes English language development, home language support, and appropriate scaffolds tailored to the needs of ELLs. Other ELLs/MLs in monolingual general education classrooms receive language support through either a push-in or pull-out model, facilitated by a TESOL-certified teacher. These services focus on vocabulary development and scaffolding during literacy and writing instruction. Students at the Entering and Emerging proficiency levels also receive additional pull-out ENL services to ensure their language development needs are met. All students, regardless of proficiency level, receive integrated ENL instruction during ELA and/or core content area instruction, with time allocations aligned to their proficiency levels. Entering students receive one unit of ENL instruction (180 minutes) through a pull-out model, along with one unit (180 minutes) of integrated ENL/ELA instruction. Emerging students receive two units of instruction: 0.5 unit (90 minutes) through pull-out ENL and one unit (180 minutes) through integrated ENL/ELA, with an additional 0.5 unit (90 minutes) allocated based on individual student needs. Transitioning students receive one unit per week, consisting of 0.5 unit (90 minutes) of integrated ENL and 0.5 unit (90 minutes) determined by student need. Expanding students receive one unit (180 minutes) of integrated ENL/ELA, which may be delivered through any content area that best supports their development. Commanding students continue to receive support for an additional two years, with 0.5 unit (90 minutes) per week of integrated ENL instruction in a content area. This comprehensive programming ensures that all ELLs/MLs receive equitable access to instruction that supports both language development and academic achievement, while also meeting state-mandated requirements. 3. How are ENL, English Language Arts (ELA) and if applicable, Home Language Arts (HLA) instructional minutes delivered in each program model? In Monolingual classes, ELLs receive their mandated minutes by a teacher that is certified in both the common branches and ESOL or by a certified ESOL teacher that works alongside a K-6 certified elementary school teacher using a push-in or pull-out method. In the Dual Language stand-alone model, a certified Elementary teacher with a bilingual extension provides instruction in both English and the home language (Spanish). All subjects are taught in both English and Spanish on alternating days. In the Dual Language side-by-side model, there are 2 certified teachers and one is bilingual certified. Students rotate and receive instruction in all content areas in English and Spanish on alternating days. In Transitional Bilingual classes, there is one K-6 certified teacher with a bilingual extension. In this program model, as the students progress in English, they will spend more time learning in English and spend less time learning in the home language. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 164 of 260 4. For schools with Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE) programs: a. Which Dual Language Bilingual Education model is implemented? Explain how much of the instructional day (percentage) English proficient students and ELLs are integrated. Elm Tree Elementary School provides a Dual Language program using the 50/50 collaborative side by side model or stand-alone for grades PK through 5th. Research shows that students in a dual language program excel and show more progress than in other programs. The target language is Spanish being that most of our ELL/ML population's home language is Spanish. The students follow an alternating schedule and students in each class are heterogeneous with regards to proficiency levels. All classroom instruction will be provided in the 2 languages (L1 and L2) and time is evenly distributed for access and equity. Scaffolding will be provided for speaking, listening, reading, and writing activities by the teacher. We implement a two-way dual language program, which is based on the premise that two groups of students (each with different home languages, one being English) learn together in a systematic way so that both groups become bilingual and biliterate in the two languages. The benefits of this programming is language-minority students build their native language proficiency, which in the long run strengthens their acquisition of the majority language (English). English speakers develop proficiency in a new language, and their English skills are strengthened by this additional cognitive process. b. In which language(s) is each core content area taught? For our Dual Language program literacy and math are taught in English and in Spanish on alternating days. Science/STEM and Social Studies are taught in English with home language supports utilized to facilitate language acquisition. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 165 of 260 5. What is the composition of the ELL subgroups at your school? What percentage of ELLs are Newcomers? How does effective instruction for Newcomers differ from those provided to Developing or Long-Term ELLs? The percentage of Newcomers (0-3 Years of Service) is 82.8% (433) students. Services for Newcomers typically focus on intensive language support and cultural orientation to help them adjust to their new environment. In contrast, Developing and/or Long-Term ELLs receive more targeted language development based on their proficiency level, with a focus on academic content and long-term language acquisition. Newcomer ELLs require intensive language support and help adjusting to a new cultural and academic environment. Instruction for these students focuses on building foundational English, especially survival language, and introducing school routines. Teachers rely heavily on visual supports, native language resources, and simplified content. Scaffolds for Newcomers include chunking texts, providing sentence frames, using real-life objects (realia), and modeling language explicitly. Students who are at Beginning Levels of Language Proficiency (Entering and Emerging) benefit from high visual support, gestures, realia, native language use, and tasks focused on vocabulary (e.g., labeling, matching, using simple sentence frames). Instruction should be highly scaffolded with multimodal inputs. In contrast, Developing ELLs have typically been serviced for 4–6 years and possess a stronger foundation in English and are at Higher Levels of Language Proficiency (Transitioning and Expanding). These students still require support, particularly in mastering academic language across subjects. Instruction emphasizes vocabulary development, reading comprehension, and structured opportunities to practice oral and written language. Teachers use small-group instruction, authentic texts, and structured peer interactions to reinforce skills. Instruction for both Newcomers with limited English proficiency and Developing English Language Learners must be responsive to their linguistic and cultural backgrounds as well as their individual academic needs. At our school, we place an emphasis on using visual supports, structured peer collaboration, explicit language modeling, and language-building routines, particularly those that prioritize vocabulary development. Translated materials and scaffolded tasks are used to ensure access to content across subject areas. Although Elm Tree Elementary does not currently serve any Long-Term ELLs, if such students were enrolled, instructional strategies would shift to include a stronger emphasis on oral language and expressive language DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 167 of 260 development. Activities such as structured discussions, oral presentations, and the use of rubrics to assess and guide oral proficiency would be integrated into daily instruction. In addition, students would be exposed to rich language experiences that include authentic texts, purposeful conversations, and real-world applications of academic language. Teachers would also implement more advanced scaffolding techniques to meet their evolving needs. What percentage of ELLs are Students with Inconsistent/Interrupted Formal Education (SIFE)? How do you differentiate effective instruction for SIFE? PS 211 has 5 SIFE students which makes 1.0% of ENL students. We differentiate by initially assessing individual needs by conducting diagnostic assessments such as; NYSITELL, Spanish Lab, Mclass (Dibels, Lectura), IReady, and map growth so we can determine the students current academic levels, language proficiency and learning gaps. In addition, we utilize information provided by the Multilingual SIFE Screener (in the designated Home Language of the student) which is a diagnostic tool used by New York State schools to identify students with interrupted or inconsistent formal education (SIFE). We use this information to gauge students' literacy skills in their home language and it helps us devise specific support to help them in closing learning gaps. Then as the year progresses we continue to use formative assessments to monitor progress. For SIFE students we design flexible, scaffolded instruction. Support for SIFE students include programming services that align to students specific needs while specifically accelerating academic literacy skills. We prioritize foundational literacy skills like phonics, phonemic awareness, and vocabulary. The use of language supports such as visuals, graphic organizers, and modified text are provided within the content areas. Additionally, we encourage our SIFE students to participate in our before or after school intervention programs to further support their individual needs. What percentage of ELLs are Long-Term ELLs? How does effective instruction for Long-Term ELLs differ from instruction designed for Developing ELLs? At PS 211, we currently do not have any long term ELLs (7 or more years of ELL service). However, we do have 90 or 17.2% of potential long-term ELLs that have 5-6 years of service. Effective instruction for Long-Term English Language Learners differs from instruction for Developing English Language Learners in several key areas, reflecting their distinct language proficiency levels, academic backgrounds, and instructional needs. For Developing ELLs, language-focused instruction is used and an emphasis is placed on explicit language instruction, including vocabulary development, grammar, and sentence structure. Teachers use visual aids, and simplified language to make content comprehensible. Scaffolding Techniques are used where teachers provide support through sentence frames, graphic organizers, and modeling to help students produce language. For instance, using sentence starters like "I think the answer is..." can guide students in forming complete sentences. Repetition and Practice is another instructional practice that provides frequent opportunities for practice and repetition to help reinforce language skills. Activities such as role-playing, interactive games, and peer discussions are beneficial as well. For Long-Term ELLs, academic language development is important. Instruction shifts towards developing academic language proficiency, focusing on reading comprehension, writing skills, and subject-specific vocabulary. Also, critical thinking and analysis where teachers encourage higher-order thinking by engaging students in discussions, debates, and problem-solving activities that require the use of academic language. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 168 of 260 What percentage of students are former ELLs? How is your school providing mandated instruction to former ELLs? There are 31 or 3.6% former ELLs at PS 211. Even after students transition out of English Language Learner programs, it's crucial for schools to continue providing support to ensure their academic success. In compliance with New York State regulations, former ELLs are entitled to receive at least 0.5 units of study, or its equivalent, of Integrated English as a New Language (ENL) instruction per week. This instruction is delivered by a certified ENL provider and is integrated into English Language Arts (ELA) or core content areas. The ENL provider collaborates with teachers to support students within the classroom. Additionally, schools are required to monitor the academic progress of former ELLs, tracking their performance in core subjects and standardized assessments. This ongoing monitoring helps identify any areas where additional support may be needed to ensure continued academic achievement. 2. Examine all at-risk levels that might adversely affect ELLs at your school. What trends do you notice about the at-risk levels of ELLs at your school? The trends of the at-risk indicators that adversely affect Elm Tree Elementary ELLs are that a substantial portion of our ELL population (82.8%) are newcomers with 0-3 years of school experience which means many of our students are adapting to the school environment and language acquisition. Another at risk indicator is unstable living situations, 64.6% of ELL students are in temporary housing situations. This then can create additional stress and barriers to learning. Nearly 37.7% of ELLs in our community have not demonstrated sufficient progress of ELP. This reflects challenges in language acquisition despite being in our school which then impacts access to grade level content and overall academic success. On ELA and Math State Tests about 22% of our ELL students scored a level 1 or 2 this indicates that there is a significant academic risk in both literacy and math. Therefore, a majority (45.9%) of our ELL population have at least two at risk indicators and some have as many as seven. Due to these multiple risks suggests that these challenges can seriously impede academic achievement and social emotional well-being. 3. Examine all at-risk levels that might adversely affect former ELLs at your school. What trends do you notice about the at-risk levels of former ELLs at your school? At Elm Tree Elementary School, several at-risk indicators continue to impact former English Language Learners (ELLs). One significant concern is housing instability, which affects both current and former ELLs. Notably, 61.3% of former ELLs are living in temporary housing situations. Academic performance is another area of concern—former ELLs continue to struggle on state assessments in English Language Arts (ELA) and Math, with approximately 21.3% scoring at Level 1 or 2, even after exiting ELL status. Overall, 48% of former ELLs at Elm Tree Elementary have at least one at-risk indicator, with some students facing as many as five. 4. What are the home languages of ELLs with the largest representation at your school? What staff, structures, and processes does your school have to support communication and learning that values these home languages? At Elm Tree Elementary School, the majority of English Language Learners speak Spanish. In addition, we also have additional languages that are spoken in our school community inclusive of Chinese, Fulani, Nepali, Bengali, and DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 169 of 260 Arabic. At Elm Tree Elementary, our Multilingual and culturally responsive staff including teachers, paraprofessionals, counselors, office personnel, a parent coordinator, and members of the administration and Instructional Leadership Team—play a vital role in supporting our ELL community. Many are bilingual and provide targeted language support, foster cultural understanding, and ensure effective communication with students and families in their home languages. and translation tools are used strategically to support communication and affirm students’ linguistic and cultural identities. Bilingual staff serve as critical bridges between the school and families, helping to ensure that language is never a barrier to engagement. They assist with interpreting during parent-teacher conferences, translating school communications, and providing real-time language support to students in and out of the classroom. Their presence not only facilitates understanding but also creates a sense of belonging for students and families by honoring their home languages. Elm Tree also hosts monthly workshops in families’ preferred languages to promote engagement and provide ongoing support. Additionally, beginner English classes are offered to parents who wish to improve their language skills. Families are always warmly welcomed at school events, including the annual Multicultural Arts Festival, where students celebrate and showcase cultures from around the world. In addition to staff support, the school leverages translation tools, such as bilingual documents, translated digital platforms, and the Elm Tree School app with multilingual features, to ensure students and families can access information in their preferred language. These tools are integrated thoughtfully, not just for logistical communication, but also to affirm students’ linguistic identities by validating their native language as a valuable asset. Students are encouraged to draw on their home language when making academic connections, and families are invited to participate in school life in their own language, reinforcing that linguistic diversity is a strength, not a barrier. By combining all of these resources, Elm Tree fosters an inclusive environment where every student feels seen, heard, and valued for who they are. In addition, Elm Tree Elementary is proud to have a dedicated team of ENL-certified teachers who deliver specialized instruction and in-class support for ELLs. Many Spanish-speaking students participate in dual language or transitional bilingual programs, where instruction and materials are provided in both English and Spanish. For students in monolingual classrooms, language resources are made available as needed or upon request to support their academic success. Through these initiatives, Elm Tree Elementary has cultivated an inclusive environment that honors and integrates students’ home languages, enhancing both communication and academic achievement. 5. What are the racial and ethnic classifications of ELLs at your school? How are ELLs being included in planning and delivery of Culturally Responsive and Sustaining Education (CR-SE)? By ethnicity, the ELLs at our school are made up of Non-Hispanic ELL's inclusive of the following: 5.7% American Indian or Alaskan Native; 37.1% are Asian, 28.6% are Black, 28.6% are White. At Elm Tree Elementary, multilingual curriculum materials and bilingual programs are thoughtfully selected and implemented to reflect students’ cultural backgrounds and support identity-affirming instruction. These resources go beyond translation, they incorporate stories, visuals, and themes that are culturally relevant and representative of the diverse communities the school serves. By seeing their languages, traditions, and experiences reflected in the curriculum, students feel recognized and valued. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 170 of 260 Our Bilingual Programs provide students the opportunity to develop academic proficiency in both English and their home language, promoting bilingualism as an asset. This approach strengthens students’ self-esteem, honors their cultural heritage, and supports deeper content understanding. Overall, these practices affirm students’ identities, build stronger connections to learning, and foster a more inclusive, respectful school environment. In order to ensure that ELLs are being included in the planning and delivery of Culturally Responsive and Sustaining Education (CR-SE), our school uses multilingual curriculum materials that incorporate texts, resources, and examples in students’ native languages and reflect a variety of cultural backgrounds. Our literacy curriculum HMH Into Reading has a Spanish counterpart, ¡Arriba la Lectura!, that is utilized in our bilingual classrooms. The texts provided in the Into Reading myBook are diverse and culturally relevant. ¡Arriba la Lectura! combines research- driven instruction and the guidance of Spanish-literacy experts to provide comprehensive support for teachers and a measured path to growth in reading, writing, and communication for students. This curriculum allows us to build on the knowledge that our ELLs have, using an asset-based approach where students’ linguistic and cultural identities are viewed as strengths rather than deficits. Lessons are designed to draw on student experiences, while providing the background knowledge and vocabulary instruction needed to access grade level curriculum. Another way we ensure a Culturally Responsive and Sustaining Education for ELLs is by using student voice and engaging families in the school community. Teachers and staff use information from student and family interviews when lesson planning to gauge students interests that can be explored in the classroom. Whenever possible, teachers connect classroom content to students’ home cultures, making learning more meaningful and affirming. Teachers draw on students’ family and community knowledge to enrich classroom learning. Incorporating choice and student interest in the classroom helps keep students engaged in the learning process. Teachers also engage ELL families by providing outreach in the home language, translated materials, and welcoming families to the classroom for events including, but not limited to, end of unit presentations, publishing parties, holiday celebrations, and a Multicultural Arts Festival. When it comes to planning and instruction, ENL and general education teachers collaborate to plan inclusive lessons that serve both content and language objectives. Instruction is planned with language supports such as sentence frames, bilingual glossaries, and visual aids to ensure accessibility while honoring multilingualism. Students are encouraged to use all their language resources in learning, discussion, and assessment. Teachers also receive professional development on how to integrate culturally sustaining pedagogy with language development strategies in their daily instruction. 6. What trends do you notice in reviewing English Language Proficiency (ELP) growth at your school? How many students met ELP sufficient progress? How many students did not meet ELP sufficient progress? According to the most recent EDAT, 65.7% of students with Sufficient Exam Results did not Meet Progress, while 14.7% met Progress by Exiting ELL Status, 15.3% Met Annual Progress Target, and 4.3% Met Safe Harbor Cumulative Growth. Further analysis reveals that 35.1% of ELLs are currently at the Expanding level of proficiency, indicating a large group nearing transition. Additionally, 112 students are within 1–10 points of advancing to the next proficiency level. This includes 24 students close to exiting ELL status, 45 students nearing the shift from Transitioning to Expanding, 22 students close to moving from Emerging to Transitioning, and 21 students on the verge of progressing from Entering to Emerging. Modality data shows that Speaking is a consistent strength across all grade levels, with students in Grades 2 through 5 scoring above 90% in this area. First grade students also demonstrated notable strength in Reading. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 171 of 260 These results suggest that instructional practices at PS 211 effectively emphasize oral language development, particularly through the use of linking words, sequencing, and topic relevance—key components assessed in the NYSESLAT Speaking modality. Students without NYSESLAT growth data were typically either newly arrived and had only taken the NYSITELL, or were in Kindergarten, where only one NYSESLAT score is available, making year-over-year growth calculations impossible. Overall, PS 211 Elm Tree is making encouraging progress in supporting ELLs toward proficiency, with strong speaking instruction and a substantial number of students on the cusp of advancing to higher proficiency levels. After analyzing our most recent NYSESLAT data, we identified a grade-wide trend indicating that writing continues to be a consistent area of need. This has been a historically persistent pattern, especially when compared to stronger performance in other modalities. Additionally, many newly arrived students struggle with expressive language skills, particularly in speaking and writing. These findings support our instructional programming— especially within the Stand-Alone ENL component—by prioritizing oral language development for Entering and Emerging students and focusing on the academic language necessary for success in content areas. Furthermore, we must continue to strengthen our writing instruction by creating more meaningful opportunities for students to engage with and respond to grade-level texts. 7. When you look at the results of the New York State English as a Second Language Achievement Test (NYSESLAT), what stands out? What trends are there for each modality? Based on our most recent EDAT, a notable trend emerged across grade levels at PS 211 Elm Tree. For first grade students, Reading was the strongest modality, with 27 out of 87 students answering 90% or more of the questions correctly. This stands in contrast to grades 3 through 5, where Speaking consistently emerged as the strongest modality. In third grade, 37 out of 95 students scored 90% or higher in Speaking; in fourth grade, 38 out of 81 students achieved the same; and in fifth grade, 37 out of 76 students reached this benchmark. Conversely, Speaking was the lowest-performing modality for first grade students, with 30 out of 76 students answering only 15% or fewer of the questions correctly. For second grade students, both Writing and Speaking were areas of concern. In Writing, 34 out of 97 students scored below 15%, while in Speaking, 20 out of 97 students fell into the same low-performance range. As we have begun analyzing the 2025 NYSESLAT data, early trends suggest a continued pattern of Writing being a primary area of need across multiple grade levels. This consistent challenge highlights the importance of closely examining current writing instruction strategies and considering additional resources or professional development opportunities to better support multilingual learners in developing their writing proficiency. 8. What is the relationship between students’ performance on the NYSESLAT and other state assessments such as the ELA assessment, the Math assessment, and the Regents exams? At PS 211 Elm Tree, there is a strong correlation between students’ English language proficiency, as measured by the NYSESLAT, and their performance on the ELA and Math State Assessments. Students who reach higher proficiency levels—particularly those in the Expanding and Commanding ranges—are significantly more likely to meet or approach grade-level standards. This trend underscores the importance of language development as a foundation for academic success. On the ELA State Test, only Former ELLs achieved Level 3 or 4. However, 53% of these students still scored at Level 1 or 2, indicating that continued academic support is necessary even after students exit ELL status. Among current DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 172 of 260 ELLs, 100% of those at the Entering and Emerging levels and 58% of those at the Expanding level scored at Level 1. These results highlight the challenges many ELLs face in accessing grade-level literacy tasks, particularly due to limited academic language development—especially in writing. In Mathematics, 15% of ELLs—primarily those at higher NYSESLAT proficiency levels—scored at Level 3, though none reached Level 4. Meanwhile, 23% of Beginning ELLs (Entering and Emerging) scored at Level 1. This suggests that while some students are able to perform procedural tasks, many struggle with the linguistic complexity required to solve multi-step math problems, which often include word problems and abstract reasoning. Additional challenges are faced by ELLs with Individualized Education Programs (IEPs). Although many of these students are at intermediate or advanced proficiency levels, speech and writing delays continue to impact their performance on both the NYSESLAT and state assessments. Modality analysis reinforces these trends. Speaking remains a consistent strength across many grade levels, while Writing continues to be a significant area of need. These patterns suggest that targeted writing instruction, clearly defined language objectives, and scaffolded academic language support across content areas are essential to improving student outcomes. Furthermore, analysis of our most recent NYSESLAT results reveals a clear correlation between language proficiency levels and performance on state assessments. Students at the Entering and Emerging levels are not demonstrating proficiency on high-stakes tests in both ELA and Math. Although there is a slightly higher expected proficiency rate in Math, performance in both subject areas remains below the academic proficiency needed for success. In summary, as students’ English proficiency improves, so does their performance on ELA and Math assessments. However, the majority of ELLs—especially those at lower proficiency levels—require sustained, language-rich instructional supports to fully access and succeed with grade-level standards. 9. What are trends reflected in the NYSESLAT results of ELL subgroups such as ELLs with IEPs and SIFE? What do you think is impacting the results for these subgroups? ELL students with IEPs often display slower progress in their language proficiency compared to their peers. Many ELL students remain in the Entering or Emerging levels on the NYSESLAT. This could be due to their learning challenges, accommodations/scaffolding/ differentiation within instruction, and difficulty integrating language acquisition within their academic needs. As a result, they require tailored support to meet their needs. SIFE students tend to score lower on the NYSESLAT, especially in literacy skills due to a lack of a consistent educational background. Their interruptions in schooling can result in gaps in foundational knowledge, making language acquisition harder. Other possible factors that could be impacting the results for these subgroups are emotional and social challenges. After recent analysis of our NYSESLAT, it appears that these ELL Subgroups such as ELLs with IEPs and SIFE still continue to underperform on High Stakes Assessments inclusive of the Math and ELA State Examinations. 10. Tier 1 Core Instruction 1. How does your school provide ELLs with a high-quality academic experience that leads to deeper learning? At PS 211 Elm Tree, English Language Learners (ELLs) receive a high-quality academic experience designed to meet their diverse linguistic and academic needs. Through explicit and differentiated instruction, ELLs are supported in developing both content knowledge and English language proficiency. The school offers a Dual Language Program, enabling students to build literacy in both their native language of Spanish and English. Additionally, Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) classes in grades 3–5 allow students who share a common home language to continue learning academic content in their native language while acquiring English. This approach ensures that students do not fall behind academically as they develop English proficiency. In Monolingual classrooms, language development is embedded within content instruction, allowing ELLs to learn content and English simultaneously. Teachers integrate language objectives into daily lessons, ensuring that students are exposed to academic vocabulary and structures in meaningful contexts. To support this, staff members —including general education teachers, special education teachers, ENL providers, instructional coaches, and administrators—collaborate during common planning periods and inquiry blocks to design and implement effective, engaging, and inclusive instructional practices. Ongoing professional development equips staff with strategies for ELL instruction, cultural responsiveness, and formative assessment techniques that guide instruction and monitor student progress. Targeted language instruction focuses on vocabulary development, grammar, and conversational fluency. The HMH curriculum incorporates culturally relevant texts, allowing students to connect their backgrounds and experiences to academic content, making learning more meaningful and engaging. ELLs participate in collaborative learning opportunities, peer feedback, and small group discussions, which provide authentic contexts for practicing English. School-Wide Initiatives including Chippy’s Learning Experiences in ELA and Math further support language development through interactive, hands-on activities and are devised to tailor instruction to meet the needs of individual learners. Technology integration plays a key role in supporting both language acquisition and content mastery. Students use platforms like iReady and Boost Ready, which offer personalized, adaptive lessons tailored to individual learning needs. These tools provide targeted practice in areas where students need the most support, while maintaining high levels of engagement through interactive content. Additionally, tools like Google Translate are used to enhance DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 175 of 260 communication and understanding, empowering both students and teachers to interact more effectively. PS 211 Elm Tree also prioritizes family engagement and social-emotional support. ELL families are welcomed into the school community and connected with resources through the school’s Social-Emotional Learning (SEL) team, which includes counselors who administer needs-assessment surveys to identify additional supports. New students are introduced during morning announcements, and staff receive a welcome email with the student’s photo, background, and interests to help build connections and foster a sense of belonging. ENL providers offer targeted workshops in phonics, reading, and writing, and families are invited to participate in sessions that equip them with strategies to support learning at home. Through this comprehensive, inclusive, and culturally responsive approach, PS 211 Elm Tree ensures that ELLs receive a rigorous academic experience that not only supports language development but also promotes deeper learning, academic confidence, and long-term success. 2. How does your school’s leadership team ensure ELLs have access to core instruction/curriculum? 2. How does your school’s leadership team ensure ELLs have access to core instruction/curriculum? (GAGLIARDI) Elm Tree Elementary's Instructional Team incorporates the needs of Multilingual Learners (MLs)/English Language Learners (ELLs) into our school’s instructional design, as they currently represent 61.13%—more than half—of our student population. Our team includes ENL Teachers/Coordinators, Designated Grade Leads, IEP & MTSS Teachers, Instructional Coaches, Data and Technology Specialists, and Administrative Staff, all working collaboratively to address student needs in a culturally and linguistically responsive manner. Our leadership team prioritizes support for MLs/ELLs by offering professional development through whole-staff training, grade-level planning, and inquiry meetings. We focus on implementing effective ELL strategies in the classroom and are committed to meeting students’ needs, valuing their presence, and recognizing them as fully capable learners. We strive to create a learning environment that is culturally sustaining and linguistically responsive, building on the strengths students bring from their home cultures. Staff are encouraged to become familiar with essential instructional routines for all ELD lessons, grounded in the research of Dr. Kate Kinsella and supported by resources from the Division of Multilingual Learners, including the Scaffolding Guide for English Language Learners. In addition to sharing best practices for MLs/ELLs, we have assessed the needs of our student population and implemented a district-wide curriculum that reflects the diversity of our learners. We continue to use HMH Into Reading and its Spanish counterpart, HMH Arriba la Lectura, to support our Dual Language and Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) program models. Our leadership team recognized the need for a more inclusive and comprehensive curriculum—one that is culturally responsive, aligned with ELA standards (NGLS), and includes assessments that prepare students for state testing formats and environment. Through reading and discussion of texts, students build background knowledge and engage with cross-curricular topics and standards. Staff have access to rich content, standards-based instruction, actionable assessment data, professional learning, and supplemental resources. The curriculum includes mentor texts that reflect authentic experiences, explicit instruction on building and evaluating ideas from text sets, and oral language development opportunities that foster meaningful discussions. Integrated ELD support is embedded in every mini-lesson, with differentiated scaffolds at three levels—light, moderate, and substantial—aligned with tabletop mini-lessons. With HMH, ELLs receive targeted English language development instruction to support success in core content areas, along with home language support and appropriate scaffolds. Our leadership team believes the support embedded in this curriculum will continue to strengthen MLs’/ELLs’ DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 176 of 260 language acquisition and prepare them for the 21st century. The curriculum promotes rigorous instruction, critical thinking, and inquiry skills, while aligning with Advanced Literacies that emphasize higher-order thinking, collaborative learning, and student-led discussions. Vertical alignment across grade levels supports the transfer of skills and concepts, deepens engagement with thematic units, and enhances vocabulary development—especially for MLs/ELLs. Culturally responsive instruction remains a cornerstone of our teaching pedagogy. We expose MLs/ELLs to texts that serve as both “mirrors” of their own experiences and “windows” into other cultures, fostering acceptance, empathy, and global awareness. Through our ELA curriculum and SEL resources, students develop cooperation, communication, and interpersonal skills. We also prioritize emotional support for MLs/ELLs and their families by distributing family and student surveys to gather feedback and better understand their perspectives and needs. We continuously evaluate student data, set goals, and collaborate with teachers to support both content mastery and language acquisition. Our leadership team equips staff with tools and professional development to enhance their ability to analyze data and provide differentiated support for ELLs. We emphasize a growth mindset by monitoring Rate of Progress Reports to track both grade-level and individual student growth. Overall, the school’s leadership team ensures that Multilingual Learners (MLs)/English Language Learners (ELLs) have equitable access to the core curriculum through differentiated instruction, inclusive program models (such as co-teaching and push-in support), ongoing professional development, meaningful family and community engagement, and data-driven instructional practices. 3. At Elm Tree Elementary, core content instruction is delivered through a combination of Dual Language, Monolingual, and Transitional Bilingual classes across grades K–5. Each instructional model is thoughtfully designed to support English Language Learners (ELLs) in developing academic language proficiency while meeting the rigorous expectations of the Next Generation Learning Standards. In the Dual Language Program (grades K–5), instruction alternates between English and Spanish depending on the language of the day, promoting bilingualism and biliteracy. Teachers implement a balanced approach to language exposure, integrating content and language learning through collaborative activities, thematic units, and explicit vocabulary instruction embedded across all subjects. Foundational literacy is supported through structured programs such as Fundations (K–3) and Heggerty (K–2), which build phonemic awareness and decoding skills in both languages. During Chippy's Learning Experiences, students receive small-group, targeted instruction tailored to their language proficiency levels. Literacy instruction is aligned to NGLS using HMH Into Reading and ¡Arriba La Lectura!, ensuring rigorous development in both English and Spanish. In mathematics, the enVision Math program emphasizes academic vocabulary and conceptual understanding, supporting both content mastery and language development. In Monolingual Classes (K–5), English is the primary language of instruction, with a strong focus on immersive literacy and content learning. Teachers use Fundations, Heggerty, and HMH Into Reading to build foundational and advanced literacy skills. Instruction is differentiated through small-group learning opportunities embedded within the literacy block, allowing teachers to tailor instruction to students’ individual language and academic needs. All instruction is aligned to the NGLS, ensuring that ELLs are held to high academic expectations while receiving the support they need to succeed. Transitional Bilingual Classes (grades 3–5) use the same high-quality instructional programs as the dual and monolingual models, with additional emphasis on leveraging students’ home language to build vocabulary and deepen comprehension. Teachers use small-group instruction to provide targeted language development, helping students make meaningful connections between their native language and English. Across all program models, Elm Tree Elementary integrates structured literacy programs, vocabulary-rich math instruction, and differentiated small-group instruction to foster language development and academic achievement. This multi-tiered instructional approach ensures that all students—especially ELLs—receive a high-quality academic experience that promotes bilingualism, biliteracy, and deep learning, fully aligned with the Next Generation Learning Standards. 4. ELLs with IEPs DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 178 of 260 For English Language Arts and Home Language Acquisition support, we currently use HMH Into Reading and its Spanish companion, ¡Arriba La Lectura!, which both include interactive components to support ELLs. Hand2Mind’s Math Fluency solution is a supplemental math program that provides everything you need to teach and reinforce multiple strategies that build number sense. The powerful combination of math fluency activities like Math Talks and Number Strings improve a student’s ability to think about numbers flexibly, efficiently, and accurately - building a strong foundation of mathematical reasoning for future math success. The curriculum is designed to provide systematic and engaging lessons in 8-12 minutes a day. We also use Decoder Kids Phonics to help build strong foundational skills in phonemic awareness, phonetic decoding, and word recognition using a systematic scope and sequence. Each Decodable Reader introduces letter-sound relationships and encourages students to decode sounds, blends, and practice High-Frequency words. These books also have illustrations that contribute to authentic and meaningful reading experiences. For Science, we use the Amplify Science curriculum. Amplify Science is a K-8 science curriculum that blends hands-on investigations, literacy-rich activities, and interactive digital tools to empower students to think, read, write, and argue like real scientists and engineers. Amplify Science was developed by the science education experts at UC Berkeley’s Lawrence Hall of Science and the digital learning team at Amplify. As The Lawrence’s first curriculum designed to address the new science standards, Amplify Science reflects state-of-the-art practice in science teaching and learning. Each unit of Amplify Science engages students in a relevant, real-world problem where they investigate scientific phenomena, engage in collaboration and discussion, and develop models or explanations in order to arrive at solutions. b. SIFE At Elm Tree Elementary, we are committed to providing comprehensive and responsive support for our Students with Interrupted Formal Education (SIFE). These DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 179 of 260 methods help bridge gaps in prior schooling and make academic content more accessible and engaging. Instruction is designed to accelerate academic literacy and critical thinking skills, helping students catch up while also moving forward. Flexible scheduling is offered when needed to accommodate students’ real-life responsibilities and ensure they can fully engage in their learning. Beyond academics, we recognize the importance of supporting the social-emotional well-being of SIFE students. Our school counselors work closely with classroom teachers to identify and respond to students’ emotional and behavioral needs. Together, they create a safe, nurturing environment where students feel seen, supported, and valued. We are intentional about creating a welcoming and affirming school culture that honors the rich languages, cultures, and life experiences that SIFE students bring to our community. This culturally responsive approach helps foster a sense of belonging and encourages students to take academic risks and thrive. c. Newcomers At Elm Tree Elementary, we are deeply committed to ensuring that Newcomer students—those newly arrived to the U.S. school system—receive the targeted support they need to access grade-level content while developing English language proficiency. Our instructional approach is grounded in research-based strategies and culturally responsive practices that address the unique linguistic, academic, and social-emotional needs of these learners. Newcomers are encouraged to maintain and draw upon their native language as a valuable resource for learning. Academic skills such as reading comprehension, critical thinking, and content knowledge often transfer from one language to another. By leveraging students’ first language, we support their English development while promoting bilingualism and biliteracy. Instruction is designed to offer multiple entry points and learning platforms that allow students to engage meaningfully with grade-level content, regardless of their English proficiency level. To foster language development and academic engagement, teachers implement project-based learning, collaborative activities, and peer modeling. These strategies not only build academic skills but also promote persistence, resourcefulness, and self-motivation. Newcomers benefit from working alongside both English- speaking and same-language peers, which supports natural language acquisition in a safe and supportive environment. Activities such as fluency building, collaborative discussions, and think-pair-share are regularly used to encourage oral language practice and build confidence. Teachers also focus on building background knowledge, especially when content may be unfamiliar due to cultural differences. Explicit vocabulary instruction, the use of realia, and visual aids help bridge these gaps. Instructional scaffolds such as sentence frames, word banks, graphic organizers, and bilingual glossaries are used to support comprehension and expression. Cognates are highlighted to help students make connections between English and their home languages, further reinforcing vocabulary acquisition. Newcomers engage in literacy activities using grade-level texts, supported by strategies such as chunked reading, word analysis, and native language resources. Classrooms are designed as print-rich environments, featuring visual schedules, labeled classroom items, and personal vocabulary books to reinforce language learning. These tools help students build independence and confidence as they navigate their new academic setting. Technology plays a vital role in supporting Newcomer students’ language development and access to academic DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 180 of 260 content. Students use tools like Google Translate to facilitate real-time translation, enabling them to understand instructions, participate in discussions, and communicate with teachers and peers. Adobe tools support multimodal learning by allowing students to create and interact with visual and written content. Additionally, adaptive programs such as i-Ready and mCLASS Boost provide personalized instruction in reading and math, targeting each student’s specific skill level. These platforms offer engaging, interactive lessons that reinforce foundational skills and promote independent learning. Upon arrival, Newcomer students are welcomed with a guided tour of the school by a staff member, during which they learn the English names of key locations. This orientation helps students feel safe and comfortable, which is essential for academic engagement. Students are also placed in small, homogeneous groups based on similar needs and meet daily with a certified ENL teacher. These sessions focus on targeted phonics and language instruction using resources such as Heggerty, Fundations, Benchmark HELLO!, and the potential pilot of English 3D. ENL teachers use a variety of effective strategies, including visual aids, front-loading vocabulary, modeling, and building background knowledge. Newcomers also have the opportunity to participate in an after-school program led by a certified ENL teacher using the Ready, Set, Go! Newcomer kits. These kits provide age-appropriate, visually supported content to build life skills, phonics, social communication, and academic language through activities in listening, speaking, reading, and writing. d. Long Term ELLs Although Elm Tree Elementary currently does not have any identified Long-Term English Language Learners (LTELLs), we are proactive in implementing instructional strategies designed to prevent students from becoming LTELLs and to support those who may be at risk. Our approach ensures that all multilingual learners have equitable access to grade-level content while continuing to develop their English language proficiency. To support potential LTELLs, we provide rigorous, standards-aligned instruction that builds on students’ background knowledge and leverages their home language as a resource for learning. In our Dual Language programs, a bilingual/ELA literacy block is implemented to provide structured support in both English and the students’ native language. This dual-language approach reinforces literacy development and strengthens comprehension across both languages. Across all content areas, teachers develop and integrate language and literacy objectives to ensure that students are acquiring the academic language necessary for success. Instruction is scaffolded through teacher modeling, visual and verbal supports, and explicit instruction in grammar and academic vocabulary. These strategies help students access complex texts and tasks while building the language skills needed to engage with grade-level content. We also emphasize oral language development through structured discussions, presentations, and cooperative learning opportunities. Students participate in partner work, flexible grouping, and peer collaboration to practice meaningful language use in authentic contexts. Teachers use rubrics and checklists to assess progress and guide next steps in instruction, ensuring that students are developing both fluency and accuracy in spoken English. We identify potential At-Risk students by analyzing their current NYSESLAT results in conjunction with their Years of Service as English Language Learners. A key focus is evaluating how much progress they’ve made within their current proficiency level and how many points they are from reaching the Commanding level. To monitor their growth, we use a multi-tiered approach that includes annual proficiency data, content-area achievement, and formative assessments that provide real-time insight into both language and academic development. Additionally, we provide targeted small group instruction to support integrated content and language learning, along with focused interventions in literacy and math. By identifying students early and implementing strategic, data-driven supports, we aim to accelerate their progress and reduce the risk of long-term ELL designation. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 181 of 260 5. What supports does your school provide to ELLs with IEPs to achieve their IEP goals and attain English language proficiency within the least restrictive environment? At Elm Tree Elementary, we are committed to ensuring that English Language Learners (ELLs) with IEPs receive the individualized support they need to thrive academically and linguistically within the least restrictive environment. Our inclusive instructional models are designed to provide access to grade-level content while addressing both language development and special education needs. ELLs with IEPs are primarily supported through Integrated Co-Teaching (ICT) classes available at every grade level. In our stand-alone ICT model, a bilingual special education teacher remains with the class throughout the instructional day. This consistent presence allows students to learn alongside their general education peers while receiving targeted supports, modified entry points, and scaffolded instruction. Teachers use ELL strategies such as academic vocabulary development, visual supports, and cooperative learning to ensure all students can access rigorous content. In our Self-Contained 12:1:1 Classrooms, ENL teachers provide push-in support, collaborating with special education teachers to deliver scaffolded instruction using evidence-based ELL strategies. These include explicit vocabulary instruction, real-life connections, and the use of manipulatives to make content comprehensible. IEPs are regularly reviewed and updated through collaboration among general education teachers, ENL providers, service providers, and families to ensure students are making progress toward their goals. In monolingual ICT settings, TESOL-certified teachers provide mandated ENL services. These educators implement scaffolds such as visuals, manipulatives, and targeted vocabulary instruction to accelerate English language acquisition. Instruction is differentiated and aligned with both IEP goals and language proficiency targets, ensuring that students receive meaningful access to content. To address reading challenges, we offer intensive small-group interventions for ELLs at risk, delivered through our Multi-Tiered System of Supports (MTSS). These interventions are tailored in duration and intensity based on student data and focus on the five essential components of reading: phonological awareness, phonics, fluency, vocabulary, and comprehension. Programs such as Sounds Sensible and SPIRE are used to deliver explicit, research-based instruction. Progress monitoring data informs groupings and instructional decisions. Instruction for ELLs with disabilities is carefully tailored to address both their special education needs and their stage of second language acquisition. Teachers and ENL providers collaborate to align instructional strategies with students’ developmental and linguistic profiles. This collaboration ensures that instructional modifications are appropriate and effective, supporting students in meeting their IEP goals while advancing in English proficiency. To enhance access and engagement, a variety of instructional modifications and technology tools are used. These include Smart Boards and interactive slides for visual learning, as well as access to Chromebooks, iPads, picture dictionaries, bilingual glossaries, and digital prompting tools. Online platforms with accessibility features—such as Immersive Reader, text-to-speech, enlarged text, and color-coding—support both in-school and at-home. Additional strategies include simplifying texts with short, clear phrases, embedding hints or reminders, and using real-life examples to explain content. Teachers may read assignments or tests aloud, pair students with same- language peers, and provide native language support when appropriate. Instruction is further supported through task analysis, visuals, extended time, and explicit instruction in study skills and strategies for using learning materials effectively. 6. How is home language assessed in each program model (DLBE, TBE, and ENL)? DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 182 of 260 At Elm Tree Elementary School, we are committed to supporting multilingual learners through intentional use of home language assessments and instructional supports across our Spanish Dual Language (DL), Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE), and English as a New Language (ENL) program. These programs are designed to ensure that students develop academic proficiency in both English and their home language while accessing grade-level content. In our DL and TBE programs, students benefit from instruction and assessment in both English and Spanish. In the TBE program, assessments are often administered in the home language to accurately gauge students’ understanding of academic content while they are still developing English proficiency. This approach ensures that students’ content knowledge is measured independently of their English language development, allowing for more accurate instructional planning. For literacy instruction, we use Houghton Mifflin Harcourt Into Reading and ¡Arriba La Lectura!—a comprehensive biliteracy solution aligned with the science of reading and designed to support multilingual learners. These programs offer robust digital and print assessments, high-interest culturally relevant texts, and scaffolded instruction that supports both whole-group and small-group learning. Teachers use the data from these assessments to differentiate instruction, monitor progress, and identify learning gaps. To complement our core literacy programs, we use mCLASS, which includes both English (DIBELS) and Spanish (Lectura) assessments. mCLASS Lectura is a universal screener grounded in modern Spanish literacy research and provides parity with English assessments, allowing teachers to track reading development in both languages. These tools help accelerate reading growth by offering actionable data that informs instruction and intervention. In mathematics, instruction is delivered in both English and Spanish on alternating days using enVision Mathematics and enVision Español (Pearson). This bilingual approach ensures that students receive grade-level instruction in both languages. ELLs receive additional support through small-group instruction and the use of targeted strategies such as graphic organizers, word walls, bilingual glossaries, modeling, and explicit vocabulary instruction. These strategies help bridge language gaps and ensure students can access rigorous math content. Technology plays a key role in supporting home language development and assessment. Students use platforms such as i-Ready Reading and Math, Epic!, and Reading A-Z, which offer content in both English and Spanish. These platforms provide interactive, adaptive practice that reinforces foundational skills and vocabulary. Assessments embedded within these programs, along with those from HMH, mCLASS, and MAP Growth, yield valuable data on students’ home language proficiency. This data is used strategically to inform instruction, monitor progress, and plan targeted interventions. In our ENL program, students receive support in listening, speaking, reading, and writing through small-group instruction and multisensory activities. While the primary focus is on English language development, home language supports are integrated to reinforce comprehension and build connections between languages. Teachers use mentor texts, shared reading, and vocabulary-rich instruction with visual and oral language supports to scaffold learning for ELLs. To ensure a comprehensive understanding of student progress, we also analyze data through the EDAT (English Data Analysis Tool). This tool synthesizes information from the NYSITELL, NYSESLAT, ELA and Math State Exams to identify at-risk students and guide instructional planning. By combining home language assessments with English language data, we ensure that all multilingual learners receive the support they need to thrive academically and linguistically. Describe systems and structures for supporting co-teaching and collaboration among teachers for all ELL program models available at your school (e.g. integrated co-teaching, Dual Language Bilingual Education teacher partnerships, etc.). At Elm Tree Elementary, collaboration among teachers of English Language Learners (ELLs) and Multilingual Learners (MLs) is a foundational practice supported by well-established systems and structures. From the beginning of the school year, all classroom teachers and ENL providers are given dedicated, weekly grade-wide inquiry periods, in addition to at least one common planning period a week. These sessions are used to analyze student data and work samples to identify problems of practice. Once a focus area is determined, teams collaboratively explore instructional strategies or methods to address the issue. After a 6–8-week implementation period, teachers reconvene to review student progress, reflect on the effectiveness of the strategy, and determine next steps. ENL providers contribute valuable insights from their small and whole group instruction, helping classroom teachers better understand student progress and make informed decisions about supports, services, or potential evaluations. To further support inclusive practices, Integrated Co-Teaching (ICT) teachers receive an additional prep period each week specifically for planning and implementing IEPs. This ensures that students with disabilities receive instruction aligned to their goals and that their progress is closely monitored. Teachers in ICT and Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE) classes also benefit from a weekly common planning period. During this time, co-teachers collaborate on lesson planning, instructional strategies, and family communication, with a focus on integrating content and language development in both English and Spanish. ENL providers are also encouraged to participate in parent engagement meetings and conferences to ensure families receive language-accessible communication and a comprehensive understanding of their child’s language development. Professional learning is a key component of our collaborative culture. Teachers receive training on co-teaching models, effective collaboration techniques, and evidence-based strategies for supporting ELLs/MLs. Instructional data is accessible to all staff through a shared digital platform, and teachers use this data to inform instruction and interventions. A shared Google Drive is used to house lesson plans, curriculum maps, and instructional resources, promoting transparency and consistency across classrooms. At the start of the school year, co-teaching teams establish clear roles and responsibilities to ensure effective collaboration. Administrators conduct check-ins with co- teaching teams to provide support, address challenges, and ensure the partnership remains productive and student- centered. New teachers are paired with experienced mentors who provide guidance, share best practices, and model effective strategies for working with ELLs. Administrators conduct both formal and informal walkthroughs to offer feedback and support throughout the year. Teachers are also encouraged to participate in inter-visitations, allowing them to observe colleagues’ classrooms and learn from successful instructional practices. These visits often highlight effective small-group instruction in literacy and math, where teachers and ENL providers collaborate to meet the diverse needs of ELLs at varying language proficiency levels. By putting these systems and structures in place, Elm Tree Elementary fosters a culture of collaboration and shared responsibility. This approach enhances the quality of instruction and ensures that all ELLs/MLs—regardless of program model—receive the targeted, high-quality support they need to succeed academically and linguistically.
