At Elm Tree Elementary, our Annual Professional Learning Plan is thoughtfully designed to equip all staff members— including teachers, paraprofessionals, and administrators—with the tools and strategies necessary to meet the academic and social-emotional needs of our Multilingual Learners (MLs)/English Language Learners (ELLs). The plan is grounded in a commitment to equity, inclusion, and culturally responsive teaching practices that foster both language development and student well-being. Each year, staff members are invited to complete interest surveys to help guide the selection of professional learning topics. This ensures that the training provided is relevant, responsive, and aligned with the needs and preferences of our educators. Professional development sessions focus on practical, research-based strategies that can be embedded into content-area instruction. These include scaffolding techniques that support the four language domains—listening, speaking, reading, and writing—while also building academic vocabulary and promoting access to rigorous content. In addition to academic strategies, our professional learning plan emphasizes the importance of creating culturally responsive and emotionally supportive classroom environments. Educators are introduced to group-based activities that promote positive peer relationships, such as morning meetings and structured collaborative tasks. These practices help students feel safe, valued, and empowered to take academic risks. Specific sessions are also dedicated to supporting newcomer students and fostering inclusive classrooms that honor students’ diverse cultural and linguistic backgrounds. To ensure a cohesive and school-wide approach, professional learning opportunities are extended to all staff and are designed to build a shared understanding of effective practices for MLs/ELLs. Topics explored throughout the year include: NYSITELL administration, scaffolding texts for MLs, teaching academic vocabulary, writing content and language objectives, and instructional approaches to boost comprehension. Sessions such as “Building Schema for Multilingual Learners,” “Empowering Educators with FURBY Features,” and “Culturally and Linguistically Attuned: Harnessing Data for Responsive Literacy Supports” provide educators with actionable strategies and tools. We also offer specialized training sessions led by experts in the field, including Dr. Kate Kinsella, whose workshops focus on equipping ELLs with vocabulary knowledge, writing skills, and effective communication strategies. Additional sessions include SPIRE training, Adobe Express virtual workshops, and district-wide events such as the Multilingual Futures Conference and the ELL Annual Parent Meeting. These opportunities ensure that staff remain current with best practices and emerging tools to support multilingual learners. To support data-driven instruction, educators are trained in the use of tools such as the EDAT (English Data Analysis Tool), which synthesizes data from NYSITELL, NYSESLAT, and state assessments to identify at-risk students and inform instructional planning. This data is used in conjunction with classroom observations and formative assessments to tailor instruction and interventions that meet the unique needs of each learner. 10. Our school has developed a comprehensive professional development plan to ensure compliance with the Continuing Teacher and Leader Education (CTLE) requirements outlined in CR Part 154, specifically regarding English Language Learners (ELLs) and Multilingual Learners (MLs). In accordance with state mandates, all certified educators—including those holding professional certificates in classroom teaching, educational leadership, and Level III teaching assistant certificates—are required to complete 15% of their CTLE hours in professional learning focused on language acquisition and best practices for supporting ELLs. Additionally, ENL and Bilingual Education teachers must complete 50% of their CTLE hours in ELL-specific professional development. To meet these requirements, our school offers a variety of targeted professional learning opportunities. These include workshops and training sessions on co-teaching strategies, scaffolding instruction, home language development, and effective practices in dual language and ENL settings. Teachers also engage in peer-led presentations, collaborative planning, and grade-level inquiry groups to deepen their understanding and share strategies with colleagues. Staff have access to professional development provided by the Division of Multilingual Learners, including asynchronous, pre-recorded sessions, as well as offerings from the Regional Bilingual Education Resource Network (RBERN) at Fordham University, Elevated Education, District 24, the UFT Teacher Center, and instructional partners such as mCLASS and HMH. These opportunities are regularly communicated through our School-Wide Weekly Wrap-Up Newsletter and direct email invitations. To monitor participation and ensure compliance, a professional learning survey is distributed annually, allowing teachers to report and reflect on the sessions they have attended. The leadership team reviews this data to ensure that all staff are on track to meet their CTLE requirements. Through this structured and supportive approach, we ensure that our educators are well-equipped to meet the diverse needs of our MLs/ELLs and maintain their professional certification. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 186 of 260 Assessment SCREENERS 11. Which screening assessments (e.g.iReady, MAP Growth, Acadience, or STAR Reading) does your school use to guide instructional planning for your ELLs? At Elm Tree Elementary, screening assessments are a foundational component of our instructional planning process for English Language Learners (ELLs). These assessments provide educators with critical data to understand each student’s language proficiency, literacy development, and academic readiness. When students in grades K–5 enter the school and are identified as ELLs, they are first assessed using the New York State Identification Test for English Language Learners (NYSITELL). This state-mandated screening tool establishes a baseline of English language proficiency across listening, speaking, reading, and writing. The results are used to determine eligibility for ENL services and to design appropriate instructional groupings, schedules, and supports tailored to each student’s needs. For students whose home language is Spanish, we also administer the Spanish Language Assessment Battery (SLAB). This tool evaluates listening and speaking skills in Spanish and provides a more complete picture of a student’s bilingual language development. To assess foundational literacy skills, all K–5 students participate in mCLASS assessments, which include DIBELS for English and Lectura for Spanish-speaking students. These assessments evaluate the Five Pillars of Literacy— phonemic awareness, phonics, fluency, vocabulary, and comprehension—and help pinpoint specific areas where students may need targeted support. The data from mCLASS is used to inform small-group instruction in both languages and to guide the planning of our Chippy Learning Experiences, which are structured literacy blocks focused on specific domains of reading development. In addition to foundational literacy assessments, we use the i-Ready Diagnostic Assessments in both English and Spanish to further identify student strengths and areas of need. These adaptive diagnostics provide detailed insights into students’ performance in reading and math, allowing teachers to differentiate instruction and provide targeted interventions. The i-Ready platform also supports tiered instruction by aligning student performance data with the Next Generation Learning Standards, ensuring that all learners receive instruction that is both rigorous and responsive. To monitor academic progress in mathematics, we administer MAP Growth assessments in grades K–5 for math and in grades 3–5 for reading. These adaptive assessments provide precise data on each student’s instructional level, whether they are performing below, at, or above grade level. The results are used to tailor instruction for individuals and small groups, ensuring that students receive the support they need to grow academically. In our bilingual math classrooms, we also use EnVision Math assessments at the end of each unit. These assessments are administered in both English and Spanish, supporting bilingual learners in developing mathematical understanding in both languages. The data collected from these assessments informs instructional decisions and ensures that students are progressing in both content knowledge and language development. To benchmark reading growth and align instruction with curriculum goals, we utilize the HMH Reading Growth Measure. This adaptive, research-based assessment connects directly to the HMH Into Reading curriculum, DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 187 of 260 providing teachers with actionable insights into students’ reading development. The tool helps educators align instruction with students’ needs and track progress over time. Together, these assessments form a comprehensive, multi-layered system that allows educators at Elm Tree Elementary to monitor student progress, differentiate instruction, and provide targeted support for ELLs. By using both English and home language assessments, we ensure that all multilingual learners receive equitable, data- informed instruction that supports their academic achievement and language development. 12. For all grades, list and describe your targeted intervention programs for ELLs in ELA, Math, and other core content areas (specify ELL subgroups targeted). Include the language(s) in which the intervention services are offered. At Elm Tree Elementary, targeted interventions for English Language Learners (ELLs) are guided by a comprehensive, data-driven approach. Multiple assessment tools—including mCLASS, i-Ready, and MAP Growth— are used to inform Tier II and Tier III instructional groupings in both ELA and Math. These data sources allow educators to identify specific skill gaps and tailor interventions to meet the diverse needs of ELLs across proficiency levels, including Newcomer, Developing, SIFE (Students with Interrupted Formal Education), and Former ELLs. In Grades K–2, foundational literacy interventions begin with the implementation of Heggerty, which supports small groups of students with gaps in phonemic awareness and early phonics skills such as the alphabetic principle and basic decoding. For students requiring more intensive support, Tier III interventions include Sounds Sensible (Grades 1–2) and SPIRE (Grades 2–5), both of which provide structured, explicit phonics instruction. These programs are delivered in English and are particularly effective for Developing ELLs and SIFE students who need systematic decoding support. Newly arrived Newcomers typically do not receive immediate intervention, as their initial focus is on acquiring foundational English language skills. To further support literacy development, the school offers an extended day phonics program targeting two key groups: (1) students approaching grade-level expectations who need additional practice in phonemic awareness and phonics, and (2) students significantly below grade level who already receive in-school interventions but require further reinforcement. This program includes ELLs across all proficiency levels and extends the instructional day by one class period. Instruction is delivered in English, with home language support provided when the teacher is bilingual. The I-Ready Diagnostic is a cornerstone of the school’s intervention strategy. Administered three times per year, it identifies each student’s academic strengths and areas for growth in both reading and math. Based on diagnostic results, I-Ready delivers personalized online lessons and provides real-time data to inform instruction. Teachers also use I-Ready’s small group and one-on-one instructional resources in Tier II and Tier III settings. This platform supports the school’s MTSS framework and is particularly effective for Newcomer, Developing, and SIFE ELLs. The program is available in both English and Spanish, ensuring accessibility for bilingual learners. For early literacy, DIBELS (English) and Lectura (Spanish) are used as universal screeners and intervention tools. These assessments evaluate foundational reading skills and provide a comprehensive view of bilingual students’ literacy development. Tiered intervention is structured as follows: Tier I includes universal screening for all students; Tier II provides targeted small group instruction for students with moderate risk; and Tier III offers intensive, individualized support for students with significant reading difficulties. These tools are especially valuable for monitoring progress among Newcomer, Developing, and SIFE students. In mathematics, targeted interventions are provided using the HMH Do The Math program, which is designed for struggling students in Grades 1–5+. This program includes 13 scaffolded modules focused on whole numbers and fractions, with built-in assessments every fifth lesson and at the beginning and end of each module. Instruction is DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 188 of 260 delivered in English, with Spanish language support provided as needed. Do The Math is used to support ELLs across all proficiency levels, including Entering, Emerging, Transitioning, Expanding, and Former ELLs, as well as Newcomer and SIFE students who require foundational math support. Together, these targeted interventions form a cohesive, multi-tiered system that ensures all ELLs receive the academic and linguistic support they need to succeed. By aligning instruction with assessment data and tailoring supports to specific ELL subgroups, Elm Tree Elementary provides equitable, high-impact instruction across all core content areas. 13. For all grades, describe how your school uses data to guide instruction for ELLs within a Multi- Tiered System of Supports (MTSS). Refer to the Instructional Leadership Tool for MLs and ELLs and MTSS Guide for MLs and ELLs to help in the development and implementation of your school’s plan for MLs/ELLs. At Elm Tree Elementary, our Multi-Tiered System of Supports (MTSS) framework is a cornerstone of our instructional approach, ensuring that all students—especially English Language Learners (ELLs)—receive the academic and language support they need to thrive. This framework is grounded in a comprehensive, data-informed process that enables educators to differentiate instruction, monitor progress, and respond to student needs in real time. The school year begins with universal screening assessments to establish a baseline for all students. We use mCLASS to assess foundational literacy skills in grades K–5 and MAP Growth to evaluate math (K–5) and ELA (grades 3–5). These assessments help identify students who may require Tier II (targeted small group) or Tier III (intensive individualized) interventions. Following the Beginning-of-Year (BOY) assessments, teachers participate in structured data inquiry meetings to analyze results, identify trends, and group students by skill level. Instruction is then adjusted through differentiated small-group learning and targeted interventions. For example, mCLASS data informs our Chippy ELA Learning Experiences, which are aligned with the Science of Reading, while MAP Growth data guides flexible math groupings and instructional planning. Students receiving ENL or MTSS services are supported during designated intervention blocks built into the school schedule. Progress monitoring is ongoing, with biweekly mCLASS checks used to evaluate the effectiveness of interventions and make timely instructional adjustments. At the end of the year, End-of-Year (EOY) MAP data is used to assess student growth and inform planning for the following school year. This cyclical process of assessment, reflection, and adjustment ensures that instruction remains responsive and aligned to student needs. Tier II supports are designed for ELLs who require additional assistance beyond core instruction. These students receive targeted small-group interventions based on formative assessment data, addressing both academic and language development needs. Evidence-based programs such as Sounds Sensible and SPIRE are implemented to support phonics and decoding skills. For students who do not demonstrate sufficient progress in Tier II, Tier III offers more intensive, individualized support. Diagnostic assessments and language proficiency data are used to identify specific areas of need and guide the development of personalized instructional plans. To ensure accurate placement and effective intervention, educators use the ELL Decision Tree, a tool that helps match supports to the unique needs of ELL subgroups, including Newcomers, Developing ELLs, and SIFE (Students with Interrupted Formal Education). Across all tiers, data-driven decision-making is central to our MTSS process. Teachers, ENL providers, specialists, and administrators collaborate regularly to analyze data and make informed instructional decisions. All data is interpreted through a culturally and linguistically responsive lens to ensure that interventions are appropriate and equitable for ELLs from diverse backgrounds. This collaborative approach ensures that instruction is not only targeted but also inclusive and affirming of students’ identities. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 189 of 260 At the end of each academic year, we conduct comprehensive outcome evaluations using summative assessments and other data points to assess the effectiveness of our instructional programs and support systems. These evaluations inform adjustments to curriculum, intervention models, and professional development priorities. Through this structured and responsive MTSS framework, Elm Tree Elementary remains committed to advancing the academic success and language development of all multilingual learners. FORMATIVE 14. Which formative assessments (e.g. NYC performance Tasks, ELL Periodic Assessment, HLA Assessment, DRA, teacher-created assessments, etc.) are used to inform and drive instruction? Formative assessments for English Language Learners (ELLs) are essential tools that provide ongoing insights into students’ language development and guide instructional decisions. To evaluate and support early literacy development among our ELL students, we begin by identifying each learner’s current English language proficiency and foundational literacy skills through classroom-based observations and formative assessments. For students in grades 1 through 5, instructional planning is guided by performance levels and modalities from the NYSESLAT, as well as district-aligned assessments aligned to the Five Pillars of Literacy. Teachers often use observation-based strategies such as anecdotal notes, checklists, and rubrics to monitor students’ language use and literacy behaviors in real time. Student work samples, including interactive notebooks, graphic organizers, and writing journals, offer tangible evidence of learning and language application. Oral language assessments like think-pair-share, retelling, and structured sentence frames support speaking and listening skills while encouraging active participation. To foster student ownership of learning, self-assessment tools and exit tickets help students reflect on their progress and set personal goals. Visual and interactive tools, including picture prompts further engage ELLs in demonstrating understanding in creative and accessible ways. These varied formative assessments not only inform instruction but also empower students to take an active role in their language learning journey. Throughout each instructional unit, students engage with clearly defined learning targets and success criteria, which are shared and discussed at the beginning of lessons. These targets help students understand what they are learning and why it matters. Teachers provide opportunities for students to reflect on their progress through learning journals, goal-setting activities, and peer discussions. These reflective practices encourage students to take ownership of their learning and recognize their growth over time. At the end of each HMH unit, students’ reading and writing are evaluated using rubrics. This data, along with real- time observations and anecdotal notes, informs small-group instruction tailored to students’ specific literacy needs. Teachers offer timely, specific feedback during lessons to support student growth and adjust instruction responsively. This dynamic approach ensures that instruction remains student-centered, culturally responsive, and focused on continuous improvement. 15. SUMMATIVE 16. Which summative assessments (e.g. NYSESLAT, ELA, Math, Regents, etc.) are used to evaluate the effectiveness of your ML/ELL programs and how does it inform instructional design and curricular decisions? (Refer to the ELL Data Analysis Tool). To evaluate the effectiveness of our Dual Language, Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE), and Monolingual Programs for Multilingual Learners (MLs), we implement a comprehensive system of summative assessments. These include NYC MAP Growth, i-Ready, mCLASS, HMH Into Reading/¡Arriba la Lectura! module assessments, EnVision Math topic tests, NYSESLAT, and the New York State ELA and Math Examinations. These tools provide critical data on student performance in both English and Spanish, where applicable, allowing us to assess academic growth, identify learning gaps, and evaluate the impact of instructional models. By analyzing this data, we are able to detect trends across grade levels, determine areas in need of remediation, and highlight instructional strengths. This analysis directly informs instructional planning, small group formation, and curriculum refinement. Data is shared with staff through professional development sessions and digital platforms, ensuring that teachers can make informed, data-driven decisions to meet the diverse needs of their students. A central component of our program evaluation is the NYSESLAT, which measures English language proficiency annually. To deepen our understanding of student growth across different program models, we analyze NYSESLAT results using the FURBY Tool, developed by RBERN, in conjunction with the EDAT Report. This dual analysis enables us to disaggregate data by program type, monitor progress, and evaluate the effectiveness of our language instruction models. The insights gained from this process support strategic decision-making around program design, resource allocation, and instructional alignment. Our MTSS Literacy Intervention framework is also informed by summative and benchmark assessment data. Students performing significantly below benchmark on mCLASS are identified for targeted intervention and are progress monitored using specific subtests, such as Nonsense Word Fluency, to track growth. Interventions include Sounds Sensible, which builds foundational literacy skills, and SPIRE, a structured, multisensory program that DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 191 of 260 supports students needing more intensive instruction in phonics, fluency, vocabulary, and comprehension. For Spanish-speaking students, mCLASS Lectura is used to monitor literacy development in the home language, ensuring bilingual learners receive targeted support in both languages. Finally, end-of-year data from MAP Growth Math and state assessments are used to identify instructional gaps and strengths across grade bands. This data supports vertical planning and collaborative inquiry among grade-level teams, enabling educators to align instruction with student needs and refine curriculum pacing and focus. Through this multi-layered approach to assessment and analysis, we ensure that our ML/ELL programs are continuously evaluated, responsive to student needs, and grounded in evidence-based practices. 17. What is your NYSESLAT administration plan? How does your school ensure that all ELLs are tested with the NYSESLAT annually? Include the titles of the staff that will administer and score the assessment. Please describe your plan to ensure that all ELLs and former ELLs continue to receive mandated instruction during the testing period. To ensure that all eligible Multilingual Learners (MLs)/English Language Learners (ELLs) are assessed on the NYSESLAT while maintaining their mandated instructional time, our school follows a structured and proactive approach. The process begins with a thorough verification of the ELL roster using our student information system to confirm that every identified student is accounted for. A comprehensive testing schedule is then developed, covering all components of the NYSESLAT and includes designated time for make-up sessions. Certified and trained staff are assigned to administer the assessments. All proctors receive training on NYSESLAT protocols, including the implementation of accommodations for students with IEPs or 504 plans. To ensure transparency and family engagement, translated notices and school-wide messaging are used to communicate the testing schedule. Additionally, we host a NYSESLAT Informational Session for families to explain the purpose of the assessment and how they can support their children at home. During the testing window, we utilize an online tracking system via our Shared Drive to monitor student participation in real time. This system ensures that no student is overlooked and that make-up testing is scheduled promptly for any absentees. Coordination with ENL and Special Education teams is ongoing throughout the process to ensure that testing does not interfere with mandated instructional services. This careful planning allows us to remain in full compliance with state requirements while prioritizing the academic and linguistic needs of our students. All constructed-response writing prompts are scored by a trained team of educators, including ENL teachers, cluster teachers, out-of-classroom staff, ICT partners, and coordinators. To maintain scoring integrity, teachers do not score the responses of their own students. This collaborative scoring process ensures consistency and fairness in evaluating student performance. Throughout the testing period, current and former ELLs continue to receive services through their respective instructional models—Dual Language, TBE, or ENL in monolingual classrooms—with appropriate scaffolds and supports embedded across content areas. This ensures that students’ academic progress is not disrupted during the assessment window. The following staff members are responsible for administering and/or scoring the NYSESLAT: Nicole Gagliardi – ENL Coordinator/Provider Jessamine McKeever – ENL Provider Mariela Lupercio – ENL Provider Mayra Puma – ENL Provider Stephanie Huerta – ENL Provider DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 192 of 260 Melissa Arbelaez – ENL Provider Rosaline Segura – ENL Provider Martha Martinez – Assistant Principal Dayana Cantillo – IEP Teacher Christina Alongi – MTSS Teacher Designated classroom staff, including ENL, content area, and bilingual teachers, are responsible for administering the Listening, Reading, and Writing components. A detailed Testing Memo outlining all logistics is shared with staff in advance for review and feedback, ensuring clarity and preparedness before the testing window opens. 18. How does your school ensure that current and former ELLs receive necessary accommodations for state assessments, including the Specialized High School Admissions Test (SHSAT) if applicable? The school is committed to ensuring that all current and former English Language Learners (ELLs) receive the appropriate accommodations necessary to fully access and participate in state assessments. To maintain accuracy and consistency, accommodation lists are carefully documented and updated in shared Excel spreadsheets managed collaboratively by ENL providers, the ENL & Testing Coordinator, and Special Education (SPED) teachers. These lists are regularly reviewed to reflect any changes in student needs or eligibility. Testing coordinators print and review these accommodations with proctors well in advance of test administration, ensuring that all staff are familiar with the required supports and testing protocols. For ELLs with disabilities, accommodations outlined in their IEPs or 504 plans are carefully implemented to meet both language and learning needs. All ELLs in testing grades receive state-mandated accommodations for the New York State English Language Arts, Mathematics, and Science exams, including extended time, separate testing locations, translated test booklets for Math and Science, and bilingual glossaries for all subjects. Families are notified in advance if their child will be receiving accommodations, reinforcing transparency and collaboration. This structured approach ensures that ELLs are equitably supported during assessments, allowing them to demonstrate their knowledge and skills without language barriers impeding their performance. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 194 of 260 Orientation details are communicated to families via the school website and a flyer sent home with all ELL students, with information translated into the family’s preferred language. During the session, members of the ENL Department, along with administrative and instructional staff, engage in discussions with families. Technology is made available to support viewing the ELL Parent Orientation video, and transcripts or translated text are provided as needed. The orientation covers the ELL identification process, program models, the ELL Family Bill of Rights, the Next Generation Learning Standards, and our commitment to access and equity for all students. A Q&A session allows families to address any questions or concerns. Parents are required to complete the ELL Parent Survey and Agreement Form within five school days of the orientation. Annual Individual Meeting to discuss goals of the program, language development progress, language proficiency assessment results, and language development needs in all content areas. Note: this meeting is separate from and does not include the mandated ELL program orientation meeting and DOE-scheduled parent-teacher conferences. Each year, we hold annual meetings to inform parents of English Language Learners (ELLs) about their child’s academic and language development progress. These meetings provide a valuable opportunity for families to meet one-on-one with school staff to discuss student performance and the services being provided. During the Annual Parent Meeting, families receive updates on their child’s English language development, results from the New York State English as a Second Language Achievement Test (NYSESLAT), and information about their child’s program entitlements. Given our large ELL population, teachers use their designated Parent Engagement Time to schedule these individual meetings, which may be held virtually or in person based on family preference. We prioritize in-person meetings, but in the event that a family is unable to attend, virtual options are provided. Language interpretation is provided for any family whose preferred language cannot be supported by on-site staff. We reference our Elm Tree Staff Language Survey, distributed at the beginning of the school year, to identify staff who speak languages other than English. When necessary, we also utilize the Office of Language Access to ensure all families receive information in their preferred language. Prior to the meetings, staff receive a memo outlining meeting requirements along with Student Data Profiles to guide their conversations. Content area teachers collaborate with ENL providers to ensure comprehensive and supportive discussions. Families are notified of the meetings through the Elm Tree School App. We are mindful of families’ varying schedules and encourage teachers to coordinate meeting times that accommodate parents’ availability. Teachers begin the meetings by clearly outlining the goals of the selected program (ENL, DL, or TBE), explaining the primary objective of the model and how the model supports the development of English language proficiency while ensuring access to grade-level academic content. They explain how the Integrated ENL model functions, including co-teaching strategies, classroom grouping, and how instruction is differentiated based on the student’s language proficiency level. Next, teachers review the student’s language development progress, drawing on classroom observations, formative assessments, and student work samples. They describe how the student is developing in the four key language DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 195 of 260 domains: listening, speaking, reading, and writing. Teachers highlight areas of strength and identify specific areas that require continued support. Teachers then share the results of the previous NYSESLAT. They explain the student’s current proficiency level (Entering, Emerging, Transitioning, Expanding, or Commanding), how that level was determined, and what it means in terms of instructional support and services. They also review the upcoming NYSESLAT and provide families with an Informational Brochure assisting families in supporting their children at home with this examination. Finally, the meeting includes a discussion of the student’s language development needs across all content areas. Teachers describe how language demands vary in subjects like math, science, and social studies, and how instruction is being tailored to support the student’s ability to comprehend and express academic content in English. They may also discuss how the student is being supported in the classrooms and what strategies are used to promote both language and content learning. All meetings are documented using a school-wide shared template, which includes the meeting date and attendees. Teachers are required to have parents sign an attendance form, and may include additional notes if follow-up is needed. All documentation is filed in the student’s cumulative record and stored in the ELL Compliance Binder for the academic year. These workshops cover a range of topics, including: connecting newly arrived families to community resources; providing translation and interpretation tools; offering guidance on accessing free English language classes; and sharing strategies for building language skills at home. We also offer enrichment activities such as socio-emotional workshops, safety town halls with the FDNY and NYPD, and classes focused on English language development and immigrant resources. In addition, we partner with Elevate Education to provide family workshops in the areas of Time Management, Home Study Environment, Goal Setting & Motivation, Stress Management, Beating Procrastination, and Conflict & Bullying. All events and workshops are communicated through our school website and communication platforms in families’ preferred languages. Attendance is tracked through sign-in sheets, and families are invited to complete surveys after each session to help us evaluate and improve future offerings. A highlight of the year is our Multicultural Celebration, where students proudly showcase artistic creations from their home countries, giving families a meaningful opportunity to engage with and celebrate the rich diversity of our school community. What culturally and linguistically responsive partnerships do you currently have that support the needs of your school community? We currently have a partnership with the Child Center of NYC, which is our Community Based Organization. Through this partnership, we are able to provide families with Workshops that are geared towards literacy development, as well as provide access to support needed by our Newly Arrived Families, The Child Center of NY's mission is to strengthen children and families with skills, opportunities, and emotional support to build healthy, successful lives. Through this partnership, we are able to provide youth development programs that enrich young people with the tools to grow into capable and confident adults. We offer afterschool programs that focus on issues ranging from health to community service. In addition, we provide workshops that cater to Adult English Classes and Health Care for families. In addition, we partner with Cool Culture. At the core of Cool Culture's work is the use of art and culture as a vehicle for transformation that engages our emotional, intellectual and political imaginations. Cool Culture believes that art is a human right, a powerful means of self-expression, and a bridge that allows us to transcend boundaries between and across cultures to build community and to increase our capacity for dialogue and understanding. Through this partnership, our families have access to a year of unlimited, free admission for up to five people to our 90 New York City cultural institution partners. Lastly, we are a part of the Cook Shop program low-income children and their families with hands-on nutrition education workshops. This free program focuses on nutrition and healthy cooking, emphasizing affordable fruits, vegetables, and whole (not processed) foods. We offer workshops for families, as well as provide in-house support for our students. Describe the structures and process in place for identification of ELLs year-round as required by the NYC DOE Policy & Reference Guide for MLs/ELLs, including your school’s identification plan for any incoming students with an Individualized Education Plan (IEP). Include: How your school proactively plans for the ELL identification process so that it does not interrupt the provision of mandated ENL and/or bilingual instruction to current, former and potential ELLs, Who is responsible for each step of the ELL identification process, and School’s plan for ensuring ELLs and former ELLs will continue to receive mandated instruction. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 200 of 260 The identification process for English Language Learners (ELLs) follows a structured, state-mandated procedure designed to ensure that all students who require language support are accurately identified and appropriately placed without disrupting mandated instruction. The process begins with the administration of the Home Language Identification Survey (HLIS), completed by parents or guardians at the time of enrollment. If the survey indicates that a language other than English is spoken at home, a follow-up interview is conducted with the student and family to gather more detailed information about the student’s language background. If further evaluation is needed, the student is administered the New York State Identification Test for English Language Learners (NYSITELL), which assesses proficiency in listening, speaking, reading, and writing (for students entering Kindergarten, only listening and speaking are assessed). Students who do not score at the Commanding level are classified as ELLs and become eligible for English as a New Language (ENL) services and, where applicable, bilingual education programs such as Dual Language or Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE). For students with an Individualized Education Program (IEP), the Language Proficiency Team (LPT)—including ENL providers, special education staff, and administrators—meets to make a final, individualized decision regarding NYSITELL participation, based on English proficiency, IEP goals, academic performance, and the impact of the student’s disability. Our school proactively plans for this process to ensure that it does not interfere with the provision of mandated ENL or bilingual instruction for current, former, or potential ELLs. Planning begins before the school year with a review of enrollment projections and preparation for HLIS administration. We coordinate closely with the registration team to ensure timely and accurate survey completion and early identification of students needing further evaluation. The ELL Identification Team at our school is comprised of school-based professionals committed to ensuring a thorough and equitable identification process. This team includes ENL teachers and the ENL coordinator, who lead the process and ensure compliance with state regulations; school administrators, who align procedures with school policies and participate in LPT meetings; bilingual special education teachers, who help distinguish between language acquisition and disability-related concerns; and MTSS providers and IEP Teacher(s) with bilingual expertise, who have been trained to administer the HLIS and conduct student interviews to evaluate language acquisition. To streamline the process, follow-up interviews and NYSITELL testing are scheduled promptly using a dedicated team of trained staff, ensuring that ENL and bilingual teachers can continue delivering instruction without interruption. A flexible testing schedule is maintained to minimize classroom disruption. Additionally, we use a shared spreadsheet housed on our school’s drive to track the status of each potential ELL in real time. This tool is accessible to ENL providers, bilingual teachers, and administrators, promoting transparency and coordination. Once a student is identified as an ELL, services begin immediately in accordance with state timelines. By planning ahead, allocating dedicated assessment staff, and maintaining clear communication across teams, our school ensures that all eligible students are identified and supported in a timely, equitable, and instructionally sound manner. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 201 of 260 Principal Certification In accordance with New York State’s Commissioner’s Regulations Part 154 as outlined and implemented in the NYC DOE Policy and Reference Guide for MLs/ELLs, I, attest that the following ELL identification procedures and activities are adhered to, and staff are trained on the items listed below. The ELL Identification Process is completed for each student within 10 school days of enrollment (20 school days for students entering with IEPs). Enrollment status of each newly admitted student is determined: a. If a student has been in New York State public schools within the past 2 years, our school contacts the previous school to obtain ELL status, relevant assessment scores, and English language proficiency level. b. If a student has been outside of New York State public schools for more than 2 years, our school administers the ELL Identification Process as part of reentry. The home language of the student is determined by a trained and licensed pedagogue. a. The parent completes the Home Language Identification Survey in the parent’s preferred language; translation services are provided by a bilingual staff member, the DOE’s over-the-phone translation services, or an alternative, high quality translation service. An interview with parents and students in English and the home language is conducted. This includes reviewing documents, prior assessments, and academic experience. An assessment of the student’s abilities and/or work samples determines the student’s literacy and math level in his/her home language. Eligibility for the NYSITELL is determined. a. If the newly enrolled student is entering with an IEP, the school’s Language Proficiency Team (LPT) recommends eligibility to the principal, who obtains final approval from the superintendent. Students are administered the NYSITELL, if eligible. Notification letters are sent to the parent/guardian in their preferred language. a. Parent is notified of their child’s ELL status and results of the NYSITELL. If the student is identified as an ELL and the student’s home language is Spanish, the student is administered the Spanish Language Assessment Battery (LAB). Student with Interrupted/Inconsistent Formal Education (SIFE) status is determined using New York State’s resources. If a student is a newly identified ELL, the parent is invited to the parent orientation meeting. a. The parent orientation meeting provides parents/guardians with an explanation of each ELL program model offered in NYC; the parent video describing the three programs is presented (in parent’s preferred language); parents/guardians are encouraged to ask questions so that they are DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 202 of 260 informed about all ELL programs; translation and interpretation services are provided throughout the meeting. ELL is placed in the ELL program that the parent selected. a. If the bilingual program that the parent selected is not available, the parent is offered a transfer to a school with that bilingual program in accordance with NYC DOE transfer policies. b. If the ELL remains in the school, the ELL is placed in English as a New Language and our school keeps track of parent selection so that when minimum thresholds are met, we can open the ELL program of choice. Parents are notified that they have 45 days to appeal the ELL status of their child (ELL Re-identification Process). If the ELL Re-identification Process is implemented for any student, the principal reviews the decision of any student whose ELL status was changed because of the ELL Re-identification Process within 6 to 12 months. Copies of documentation regarding ELL identification, including letters and parent notifications are kept in the students’ cumulative files. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 211 of 260 3. Describe the resources and processes your school will use to communicate with parents who speak a language other than English during an emergency (e.g., lockdown, fire, student attendance, nurse/hospital visit, etc.), or when a parent needs to contact the school in an emergency, ensuring communication is in their preferred language. In the event of an emergency, Elm Tree Elementary School is committed to ensuring timely and effective communication with all families, including those who do not speak English. Our first step will be to engage any available staff member who is proficient in the family's home language to make direct contact. If additional support is needed, we will utilize the over-the-phone interpretation service to facilitate accurate communication. Emergency notifications will also be distributed through the Elm Tree App (powered by EDLIO). Both platforms support multilingual communication and will deliver messages in each family's preferred language. To ensure effective use of these tools, staff will receive training on the Elm Tree App, including its language accessibility features. Furthermore, we will proactively use the over-the-phone interpretation service to inform families that they are welcome to contact the school in their native language at any time. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 212 of 260 Part D: Staff Awareness Part D: Staff Awareness Staff Awareness Plan to ensure staff awareness of language access requirements and available resources. 1. Describe how your school will ensure that all staff members complete the required asynchronous NYCPS Language Access Training for School-based Staff on the WeLearnNYC learning platform, and are familiar with the Best Practices for When to Provide Translation of Critical Written Communications to Parents and Best Practices for When to Provide Interpretation for Critical Oral Communications with Parents. Additionally, explain how your Galaxy-designated LAC will disseminate future language access resources and updates to all staff members. Elm Tree Elementary School is committed to ensuring that all staff members complete the required asynchronous NYCPS Language Access Training for School-Based Staff on the WeLearnNYC platform. To support this, the school administration will communicate clear expectations and deadlines through staff meetings and email notifications. Completion of the training will be done during one of our scheduled PL Time Frames afterschool. In addition, they can elect to complete it during their designated OPW Time Frame if they choose. Reminders will be sent regularly to ensure full participation. In addition to the training, staff will be introduced to the “Best Practices for When to Provide Translation of Critical Written Communications to Parents” and “Best Practices for When to Provide Interpretation for Critical Oral Communications with Parents.” These resources will be reviewed during professional development sessions and made readily accessible through shared digital folders, our Instructional Handbook, and printed copies in common staff areas. The school’s Galaxy-designated Language Access Coordinator (LAC) will play a key role in maintaining and disseminating language access resources. The LAC will ensure that all staff are informed of updates and new guidance from the DOE by sharing information during staff meetings and through email communications. The LAC will also serve as a point of contact for any questions related to language access and will provide ongoing support to help staff implement best practices effectively. This comprehensive approach ensures that all staff are well- equipped to communicate with and support the diverse linguistic needs of our school community. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 213 of 260 Part E: Parental Notification Part E: Parental Notification Parental Notification Provision of notice to parents on the available language assistance services. 1. Describe how your school will inform parents about available language assistance services in their preferred language. Include any of the following: Translated signage Brochures, flyers, or letters shared with parents Parent engagement events where language assistance services are highlighted (e.g., PTA meetings, international parents’ night, etc.) Messaging applications Language Access for NYC Public Schools Families brochure The Language Access Coordinator (LAC) has completed training to ensure they can provide families with accurate information about their rights to translation and interpretation services. A welcome poster is displayed at the school entrance, featuring key information and a QR code that links to additional language access resources. Informational brochures are distributed in student folders, and when in-person distribution is not possible, materials such as brochures, flyers, and letters are shared virtually through the school website. For family engagement events—including PTA meetings and other school activities—translation and interpretation services are provided through available bilingual staff, the over-the-phone interpretation line, or DOE translators, either in person or virtually, to ensure all families can participate meaningfully. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 214 of 260 Part F: Monitoring Part F: Monitoring Monitoring Methods for soliciting and implementing feedback on language assistance services. 1. In addition to the feedback received from parents via OLA’s Translation & Interpretation Feedback Survey, describe the methods your school will use to gather feedback from parents regarding the language assistance services they receive in their preferred language. The Language Access Coordinator will collect feedback on available services through various channels, including Town Hall meetings, PTA meetings, School Leadership Team (SLT) meetings, Language Access Parent Surveys, and informal conversations with families. During the 2025–2026 school year, we will continue to engage a focus group of families to identify opportunities for enhancing our current practices. Data will be gathered and analyzed to inform new goals aimed at improving the efficiency and effectiveness of our language services. Feedback will be collected both in person and remotely—via Google Forms, on-site in families’ home languages, or through the Elm Tree website. To ensure accessibility, we will submit written translation requests in advance when needed, based on families’ preferred languages. We will also utilize school-based staff and digital translation tools to accommodate all language needs. 2. How has your school worked to improve its language assistance services? Share any feedback results and outline your plans for further improvements in the upcoming school year. Family feedback has consistently shown appreciation for the accommodations provided during both in-person and virtual events. Families have expressed a strong interest in continuing to have all presentations and workshops offered in multiple languages, as needed. However, we are looking for more effective ways to provide translation for families who speak varied languages. The use of a Google Reflection Form after each event has proven valuable in helping us plan future events and better understand the needs of attending families. All workshops and presentations are translated into multiple languages and made available on our school website. Additionally, feedback has guided us to offer presentations that introduce translation resources for both families and students. These workshops include demonstrations on how to access and use translation websites effectively. Looking ahead to the 2025–2026 school year, we will continue to ensure that all presentations are available in families’ preferred languages. We will also maintain regular surveys to gather input on how we can further improve our services. The Title III supplemental instructional services must be based on student need. These supplemental services should complement core bilingual and ENL services required under CR Part 154. Direct supplemental services should be provided for before school, after school, and Saturday programs. Teachers providing the services must be bilingual education and/or English for Speakers of Other Languages (ESOL) certified teachers. High quality professional development that is "of sufficient intensity and duration to have a positive and lasting impact on the teachers’ performance in classrooms." Professional development activities should be well-planned, ongoing events rather than one-day or short-term workshops and conferences. Parent engagement and supports must ensure that there are appropriate translation and interpretation services to meet community needs. These are in addition to mandated activities, such as parent orientation during ELL identification process or annual ELL parent meetings. Note: The Title III ELL program planning ratio is as follows: 60% to direct instruction, 10% to professional development (if funded using Title III ELL funds), 10% to parent engagement (if funded using Title III ELL funds), and 20% to OTPS (if funded using Title III ELL funds). For more information on Title III requirements, please see the School Allocation Memo or contact your ML/ELL Director or ELL Compliance and Performance Specialist. PS 211 is proud to offer Title III ELL programs designed to support English Language Learners (ELLs) at all levels of proficiency. Our programming includes a before and after school ENL Program all aimed at enhancing language development and academic success. These programs are tailored to meet the needs of our diverse ELL population, with a particular focus on Newcomer students (0–3 years of service), who make up 82.8% of our ELLs, and Developing ELLs (4–6 years of service), who comprise 17.2%. We are pleased to offer four programs to support our English Language Learners for the SY 25-26. Program 1: Newcomer Support for Grades 3–5: This afterschool program is designed to support our Newcomer Population in Grades 3–5—students who are newly arrived in the United States and have scored at the Entering and/or Emerging levels on the NYSITELL. The program will run twice a week on Wednesdays and Thursdays, from 2:20 PM to 4:20 PM. Instruction will focus on explicit phonics using high-utility vocabulary, with lessons integrated across the four language modalities: listening, speaking, reading, and writing. In addition to academic instruction, the program emphasizes social language development, helping students acclimate to their new environment and communicate effectively. Approximately 10 students will participate, homogeneously grouped based on their Years of Service and English language proficiency levels. The program will be led by one bilingual-certified teacher. Given the small group size, instruction will follow a station-based model and incorporate project-based learning to maximize engagement and personalized support. Program 2: Newcomer Support for Grades K–1 (Foundational Reading, Language and Literacy): This afterschool program is designed to support our Newcomer Students in Kindergarten and Grade 1 who have 0–1 Years of Service. Students in this group demonstrate a range of English language proficiency levels—from Entering to Expanding— based on their most recent NYSITELL and/or NYSESLAT scores. In addition to language data, instructional planning has been informed by our Universal Screener (mClass), with a focus on foundational literacy skills. The program will operate three days a week—Tuesdays through Thursdays—from 2:20 PM to 4:20 PM. Approximately 15–20 students will participate. Instruction will be delivered by two educators: one Bilingual Certified Teacher and one TESOL Certified Teacher. Teachers will co-plan and parallel teach, differentiating instruction to meet the diverse needs of the group. A station rotation model will be implemented, featuring strategically designed activities that support both language development and early literacy acquisition. Program 3: Developing ELL Support for Grade 5: This before- and afterschool program is designed to support Grade 5 Developing ELLs, including students with IEPs, who are at the Expanding level of English proficiency and at risk of becoming Long-Term ELLs. The program is led by a TESOL-certified ENL teacher who also supports 5th-grade instruction during the school day, ensuring instructional continuity. Sessions take place three days a week (Tuesdays–Thursdays): Morning Program: 6:50 AM–7:50 AM and Afterschool Program: 2:20 PM–4:20 PM. Approximately 15 students in total will be attending programming, with approximately 5 students in the Morning Program and 10 students in the After Program. Instruction targets all four language modalities—listening, speaking, reading, and writing—and aligns with NYSESLAT task types. For Developing ELLs, who have not yet demonstrated English proficiency despite extended service, instruction focuses on advanced DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 219 of 260 literacy strategies. These include vocabulary development through concept maps and visuals, multisensory phonics instruction, and comprehension and writing activities using leveled texts and structured discussions. Students are also taught to use metacognitive strategies such as self-assessment checklists to monitor their progress. A station- rotation model will be used to differentiate instruction, promote academic language development, and enhance student engagement. Program 4: 1st-2nd Grade Entering and Emerging ELLs: This After School Program is designed to support students in Grades 1st-2nd who are at the Entering/Emerging Levels of Language Proficiency. This program is led by two, TESOL Certified Teachers in both Grades 1 and 2 to support with the continuity of instruction during extended day programming. The program will take place two days a week (Tuesdays and Thursdays from 2:20PM-4:20PM). ELLs, at this proficiency level, will engage with activities that are structured, inclusive of a language-rich framework that supports the development of academic vocabulary, oral language, and foundational literacy skills. Teachers emphasize structured speaking and listening, which builds confidence and fluency through partner talk, discussion routines, and modeled responses. In addition, they provide consistent practice with language structures in engaging ways, such as vocabulary games, role-plays, and scaffolded reading and writing tasks. These supports align with NYSESLAT performance indicators, promoting academic language development and accelerating ELL progress in the early grades. This also allows for ENL Teachers to transfer the methodology they use in their Stand-Alone Instruction into Extended Day Programming to support student learning. Instruction will be team-taught, however parallel techniques can be utilized as needed with a group of approximately 20 students. This flexible approach ensures that all students receive targeted support in a format that best suits their learning style. Through this comprehensive programming, PS 211 aims to provide equitable access to high-quality instruction, foster foundational and advanced literacy skills, and support ELLs in their journey toward English language proficiency. The program will be supervised by Assistant Principals Ms. Jennifer Erichsen, Ms. Michelle Pinargote, and Ms. Martha Martinez. Supervisors are not funded through Title III. Supervisors working the before and after school programs work regular hours of 7:00 AM to 3:00 PM, 7:15 AM to 3:15 AM and 7:30 AM to 3:30 PM. After school programs will run from 2:21 to 4:21 PM. Before school programs run from 6:50 AM to 7:50 AM. This funding is supported by Rationale for Title III ELL Funded Programs Describe the rationale for each Title III ELL funded program. For each program, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis that support the need for program, and Data sources used to justify the need for the program Elm Tree Elementary serves a diverse and growing population of English Language Learners (ELLs) and Multilingual Learners (MLs), with approximately 60 students enrolled in our language support programs. These students represent a range of language proficiency levels—as determined by the most recent NYSITELL and NYSESLAT assessments—including Entering, Emerging, Transitioning, and Expanding. The demographic profile of our student population highlights the critical need for these programs. According to the most recent EDUR and EDAT data, 61.3% of our student population are ELLs. Elm Tree Elementary serves a slightly higher proportion of Newcomer ELLs (82.8% than Community School District 24 at 79.7%), and a marginally higher percentage of Developing ELLs (17.2% vs. 16.7%). Additionally, our ELL population includes 19.7% ELLs with IEPs, DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 220 of 260 and 1% Students with Interrupted Formal Education (SIFE). The majority of Newcomer ELLs are programmed in Kindergarten and 2nd grade, while most Developing ELLs are in 4th and 5th grades. Grade-level distribution further highlights the need for differentiated support: 18% of ELLs are in Kindergarten, 17% in 1st grade, 19% in 2nd grade, 18% in 3rd grade, and 14% each in 4th and 5th grades. These figures indicate that a significant portion of our ELLs are in the early stages of their academic journey and will require sustained support to achieve language proficiency and meet grade-level standards. Moreover, 44.4% of our ELLs have at least two At-Risk indicators, with some students presenting as many as seven. Key risk factors include being in temporary housing (64.6%), insufficient English language proficiency progress (37.7%), and scoring at Level 1 or 2 on state ELA (21.4%) and Math (22.0%) assessments. These indicators further justify the need for targeted programs that address both academic and socio-emotional challenges. Our ELL population includes students participating in English as a New Language (ENL), Dual Language, and Transitional Bilingual Education programs, as well as students with special needs. To address the varied linguistic and academic needs of these learners, we are implementing four programming models to support our data analysis. These targeted programs aim to provide responsive and equitable instruction that supports both language development and academic achievement. A dedicated Extended Day program for Newcomer students in Grades 3–5 is essential, as many arrive with limited or interrupted formal education. At this critical stage, they need intensive, structured English instruction that targets foundational skills in listening, speaking, reading, and writing, while also addressing gaps in core content knowledge. Additionally, the program provides a culturally responsive and emotionally supportive environment to help students adjust to a new country, language, and school system—fostering both academic growth and social- emotional well-being. For students in Grades K–1, a targeted program to build foundational literacy and language skills is equally important. With 35% of our ELL population in early childhood grades, early intervention is critical to long-term success. Our Universal Screener data highlights significant need: 79% of Kindergarteners and 54% of 1st graders are well below benchmark in foundational literacy skills. A focused program in these grades ensures students develop the language and decoding skills necessary to become proficient readers. Similarly, Developing ELLs in Grade 5 require ongoing, targeted support to progress beyond the Expanding level of English proficiency. While they have moved past the basics, they are not yet proficient on the NYSESLAT and remain at risk of becoming Long-Term ELLs—a subgroup that makes up 17.2% of our ELL population. This program focuses on academic vocabulary, reading comprehension, and writing to ensure students can fully access grade-level content and are prepared for future academic success. In Grades 1–2, ELLs make up 36% of the ELL population based on the most recent EDAT. In addition, mClass data show that 54% of 1st graders and 42% of 2nd graders are well below benchmark in foundational literacy. To close these gaps and align with school and district literacy goals, a designated language development program is necessary to support early reading proficiency and long-term academic achievement. In conclusion, these targeted programs for Newcomer and Developing ELLs are critical for advancing educational equity. They provide the structured language support, academic scaffolding, and social-emotional care needed to help our multilingual learners thrive—now and into the future. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 225 of 260 Part C: Professional Development Part C: Professional Development Professional Development Summary Provide a summary of your school’s plan for professional development under your Title III ELL program. With English Language Learners (ELLs) comprising 61.13% of our student body—and 82.8% of those identified as Newcomers—our school is deeply committed to delivering targeted, data-driven support. According to the most recent EDAT, among ELL students in Grades K–5, 10.7% are at the Entering level, 21.8% at the Emerging level, 23.2% at the Transitioning level, and 34.3% at the Expanding level. Only 10% have exited ELL status in the past two years and are classified as Former ELLs. While many students are progressing through the intermediate stages of English language development, a relatively small percentage have achieved full proficiency (Commanding), underscoring the ongoing need for intensive language support. To ensure the effectiveness of our Morning and After-School programs, we are prioritizing high-quality, targeted professional development (PD) for all participating educators. These sessions will be strategically scheduled and aligned to meet the instructional needs of our multilingual learners. Key components of our professional learning plan include: HELLO! Program (Benchmark Education): Training will focus on building ELLs’ listening, reading, and writing skills through culturally responsive, research-based instruction (Our Newcomer Program for Grades 3-5) mCLASS Progress Monitoring: Educators will receive ongoing support in utilizing mCLASS to track student growth and evaluate instructional effectiveness. (All Title III Programming) Data-Driven Instruction: Teachers will learn how to analyze and apply student data to inform instructional decisions and differentiate support. (All Title III Programming) ENL Instructional Strategies: PD will include evidence-based practices grounded in the work of Dr. Kate Kinsella, emphasizing structured academic discourse, vocabulary development, and scaffolded writing to support English language acquisition. (All Title III Programming) Small Group Instruction: Sessions will focus on enhancing small group instruction through data-informed planning DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 226 of 260 and implementation. (All Title III Programming) Lexia English: Teachers will be trained to integrate this adaptive platform, which provides personalized language instruction aligned to each student’s proficiency level. (All Title III Programming) Foundational Literacy Skills: Teachers will be provided with PD connected to the Science of Reading to support them in their implementation of best practices for literacy instruction. (All Title III Programming) Unpacking the NYSESLAT: Teachers will unpack the NYSESLAT Examination to better understand the Targets of Measures within the examination in order to support them in their creation of language goals. (All Title III Programming) Elevate Opportunities: These professional learning opportunities are designed to equip teachers with the tools and strategies necessary to deliver effective, engaging, and culturally relevant instruction—ultimately driving improved student outcomes and increased language proficiency across grade levels. All professional development will be facilitated in-house at no cost to Title III funds. However, if specialized external training is needed to address specific instructional goals, Title III ELL funds will be allocated accordingly to support those initiatives. Rationale for Professional Development Describe the rationale for the professional development to be provided. For each activity, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis (e.g., patterns and trends identified) that support the need for the professional development, and Data sources (e.g., school survey teacher responses, in-house teacher surveys, teacher observation data) used to justify the professional development. According to our most recent EDAT (English Language Learner Data Analysis Tool), 65.7% of our ELL students have not yet met their annual progress targets. Many students remain below expected growth benchmarks, indicating that we must further strengthen our instructional strategies to accelerate language acquisition. Notably, the majority of our ELLs fall within the Newcomer subgroup (82.8%), which underscores the need for targeted interventions tailored to students who are new to the U.S. school system. In addition, majority of our English Language Learner population fall within the Grades 1-3, which encompasses 54% of our ELL Population. We want to ensure that students are receiving a supportive structure through our Extended Day Programming to ensure that they are able to reach English Proficiency. The EDAT also reveals that our greatest area of need lies in writing, with additional support required in speaking, particularly in the foundational grades. These findings align with teacher feedback gathered through in-house surveys and needs assessments, which indicate a strong demand for professional development focused on scaffolding strategies that enable ELLs to access and engage with academic content effectively. To address these needs, our professional learning plan will focus on equipping educators with the tools and knowledge necessary to support language development across content areas. A key component of this plan DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 227 of 260 involves unpacking the NYSESLAT to better understand current trends in student performance and identify instructional strategies that promote growth. Teachers will engage in professional learning grounded in the Academic and Linguistic Demands: Creating Access to the Next Generation Learning Standards in English Language Arts for Linguistically Diverse Learners, in conjunction with the Literacy Shifts in K–5 and our use of the mCLASS platform. This approach is rooted in the belief that language development must occur within meaningful academic contexts. Instruction will focus on helping multilingual learners develop the language structures and discourse practices specific to academic disciplines, enabling them to participate authentically in content-area learning. Through these professional development opportunities, teachers will strengthen their ability to use data to drive instruction, implement effective scaffolds, and provide equitable access to rigorous academic content. The key data points reflected above are indicative of our need to Support our Multilingual Learners in Early Childhood Grades, as well as support our Newcomer Population and Developing ELL Population. Ultimately, this work will directly impact students’ language proficiency and performance on high-stakes assessments. All professional development will be designed to build internal capacity, with in-house facilitation. Elm Tree Elementary School currently serves a student population in which 61.13% are identified as English Language Learners (ELLs). This data reflects the rich linguistic and cultural diversity of our school community, with many students coming from immigrant families who are still developing English language proficiency. To support the academic and social success of our ELLs, it is essential to engage not only the students but also their families. Research and experience show that the most impactful plans for student growth involve families as active partners in the learning process. Families are invaluable stakeholders in their children’s academic achievement and social integration. To empower families with the tools they need to support their children’s language development and educational progress, we will offer monthly family workshops. These sessions will provide guidance, resources, and strategies that families can use at home to reinforce learning and foster a supportive environment. All presentations and materials will be translated into the home languages of our students to ensure accessibility and inclusivity for all members of our school community. These workshops will be offered at no cost to Title III ELL funds when conducted during the school day. However, if sessions are held outside of regular hours, per-session compensation for teachers may be supported through Title III funding. Additionally, if external providers are needed to facilitate specific learning opportunities for families, those services will be documented and funded through Title III ELL allocations. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 232 of 260 Rationale for Family Engagement Activities Describe the rationale for the family engagement activities to be provided: For each activity, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis (e.g., reasons for topics, time activities will be offered), and Data sources (e.g., parent survey responses, in-house parent surveys, PTA/PA request, etc.) used to justify the need for activities. At Elm Tree Elementary School, 61.13% of our student population are English Language Learners (ELLs), as reported in our most recent English Data Analysis Tool (EDAT). The top three home languages spoken by our students are Spanish, English, and Chinese, reflecting the linguistic diversity of our school community. To better understand the needs of our families, we conducted an interest survey, which revealed several key findings. Many families expressed a desire for support in developing English language skills at home, not only for their children but also for themselves. Additionally, families reported that their children face challenges with reading and writing at home and struggle with motivation to complete academic tasks. They expressed interest in strategies to create a more engaging and supportive home learning environment. Families of students with special needs shared that they experience increased difficulty in supporting their children’s academic progress while also navigating the complexities of learning a new language. These insights highlight the need for targeted, inclusive programming that addresses both academic and language development in the home setting. Moreover, results from our Winter Family Survey indicated that 12.5% of families felt that some existing programs and resources were not effective in supporting their child’s learning. This feedback underscores the importance of continuously evaluating and refining our offerings to ensure that all families feel supported and empowered. In response to these data points, we are committed to designing and delivering family-centered learning opportunities that are responsive to the specific needs identified. Our goal is to ensure that 100% of families are satisfied with the resources and support provided, ultimately contributing to student success both at school and at home.
