Educational Software (Object Code 199) Travel Other TOTAL DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 237 of 260 Title III Immigrant Outline Title III Immigrant Outline Title III Application for Immigrant Funding REQUIREMENTS Title III, Part A - Immigrant Funds can be used for activities that provide enhanced instructional opportunities for immigrant students, which include: Identification and acquisition of curricular materials, educational software and technologies to be used in the program. Family literacy, parent outreach and training activities designed to assist parents in becoming active participants in the education of their children and understand their rights and the benefits of multilingualism. Support for personnel, including teacher aides, parent coordinators, and school counselors who have been specifically trained, or are being trained, to provide services to immigrant students and families. Provision of tutorials, mentoring, and academic or career counseling for immigrant students, as well as engagement in pre-college and work-based learning experiences. Basic instructional services directly attributable to the aforementioned school districts, including costs for additional classroom supplies, transportation costs, or other costs directly attributable to such services. Other instructional services that are designed to assist immigrant students to achieve in elementary schools and secondary schools in the United States, such as programs of introduction to the educational system and civics education. School activities, coordinated with community-based organizations, institutions of higher education, private sector entities, or other entities with expertise in working with immigrants, to assist families of immigrant students by offering comprehensive community services. Funding follows similar guidelines for Title III funds established by the Office of Multilingual Learners (OML) and can only be used to provide supplementary services to immigrant students, who may or may not be English Language Learners (ELLs). Please note: Immigrant students are not necessarily English Language Learners (ELLs), and ELLs may or may not be immigrants. For the purpose of this funding, immigrant students are defined as individuals who were not born in any U.S. state (this includes the District of Columbia and the Commonwealth of Puerto Rico); and have not been attending one or more schools in any one or more states for more than three full, academic years (the months need not be consecutive). DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 238 of 260 Priority areas for the use of Title III, Part A - Immigrant Funds are as follows: Extended day programming (e.g., before school, after school, Saturday program) to provide targeted, small-group instruction, credit recovery support, and postsecondary preparation. Implementing strong student supports to increase graduation rates. Community-based and school-based programming (e.g., before school, after school, Saturday program) to strengthen partnerships between immigrant families and schools. Developing new and/or enhancing existing programs for newcomers (meeting criteria defined above). Supplementing the opening or growth of Transitional Bilingual Education programs and/or Dual Language Bilingual Education programs serving immigrant students. Professional learning on meeting the needs of immigrant students, creating a welcoming and inclusive school environment, and effective instructional practices. For more information on Title III requirements, refer to NYSED’s guidance on allowable and unallowable expenditures for Title III. For specific allocation amounts per school, see the School Allocation Memo or contact your ML/ELL Director or ELL Compliance and Performance Specialist. DIRECTIONS Please complete the application below. Schools must use the funds to support their immigrant students by developing a plan that has each of the following components: Direct instruction, supplemental program for immigrant students, Professional development that bolsters the instructional practice of teachers of immigrant students, and Family engagement activities that support families of immigrant students. Completed applications must be submitted in iPlan for review and approval. Please refer to the respective School Allocation Memo. Schools are not permitted to use funds until they submit a plan and receive notification of its approval. Select all that apply. ,K,1,2,3,4,5 Total number of teachers in this program: 4 Provide a summary of each program funded under Title III immigrant for SY 25-26: For each program, describe: When the program takes place (i.e., after school, before school, Saturday academy). Activities the students will engage in and how these activities will enhance instructional opportunities for immigrant students (including ELLs). Our First Program will focus on English Language Learners (ELLs) and Multilingual Learners (MLs) who need English Language Development (ELD). This afternoon program will serve 20 students in Grades 3-5 who newly arrived in the country and hold ELL status. All 20 students in this group are immigrant students who will follow a station-rotation model. The program will utilize HELLO!, a curriculum designed to support recently arrived students by teaching essential English skills. It integrates survival English, language development, and social-emotional learning, while also embracing the use of students' primary language, prior knowledge, and cross-cultural understanding. Instruction will emphasize academic language, metacognitive strategies, and socio-affective skills. Additionally, lessons will include resources that facilitate home-school connections, enabling parents to actively participate in their child's education, which is crucial for academic success. The program is a part of the Benchmark Advance Curriculum and tracks student progress through Pre/Post Tests in Listening, Reading, Writing, and Speaking. The DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 241 of 260 teacher involved in this program is Bilingual-SPED Certified, ensuring the implementation of effective strategies for promoting English language proficiency and making connections with students' home language. Additionally, we will incorporate English Phonics Development through Decoder Kids, which helps students decode sounds and blends, fostering early reading success. The program also offers engaging fiction and nonfiction readers that provide opportunities for practicing phonics and high-frequency words in context, supporting vocabulary growth and word study. Students will be homogenously grouped based on Years of Service with one teacher who will employ Project Based Learning Opportunities and Station Teaching to enhance the student experience. Group size will not exceed 20, which allows for tailored support, better engagement, and personalized instruction in language acquisition. Instruction will include explicit, structured instructional activities such as: -Daily survival-English mini-lessons focused on real-world scenarios (e.g., navigating school routines, asking for help, engaging in classroom discussions) -Guided oral language practice through structured partner conversations, sentence frames, and role-playing activities -Interactive vocabulary development using visuals, realia, and primary-language supports to build background knowledge and comprehension -Listening and speaking routines, including think-pair-share and oral rehearsals, to promote confidence and language production A key focus of this program is supporting cultural adjustment for newly arrived immigrant students as they acclimate to U.S. schools. Instruction intentionally addresses unfamiliar school norms, routines, and expectations through daily survival-English lessons and modeled classroom interactions, helping students understand how to navigate schedules, participate in discussions, ask for assistance, and interact with peers and adults. Social-emotional learning is embedded throughout instruction to create a safe, welcoming environment where students feel valued and confident taking academic risks. By honoring students’ home languages, cultural backgrounds, and prior experiences, the program reduces cultural barriers to learning and fosters a sense of belonging, allowing students to engage more fully in instruction and access academic content with greater confidence. Our Second Program will serve students in Grades 1-2 afterschool, focusing on Essential and Creative Writing Skills. The program aims to strengthen students' abilities in written expression, starting from recognizing sentence structure to developing short stories, all while emphasizing the key elements of the writing process. Teachers will use grammar resources, such as the Sadlier Workshop, to reinforce grammatical rules as needed. Students will collaborate to revise and edit their writing, while also learning foundational writing skills that are critical to improving their writing competencies. This program will be taught by one bilingual-certified teacher, ensuring effective support for students who speak Spanish as their home language. Groupings are heterogeneous in nature and comprised of approximately 20 ELL's at various English Language Learner levels, all of which are Immigrant Students. Instruction encompasses station rotations with one designated teacher who circulates and facilitates support for students. Instructional activities will include: -Modeled and shared writing lessons focused on sentence structure, grammar and idea development -Guided writing practice using sentence frames, word banks, and graphic organizers -Small-group and partner revision activities to strengthen editing and collaboration skills This afterschool writing program enhances instructional opportunities for immigrant students in Grades 1–2 by providing explicit, structured instruction in essential and creative writing skills. By teaching sentence structure, storytelling, and the writing process, students gain familiarity with academic writing expectations, supporting their adjustment to English-based classroom instruction. Cultural adjustment is supported through collaborative writing, peer discussions, and shared revision activities that promote participation practices such as turn-taking, feedback, and group learning. The bilingual-certified teacher validates students’ home language and experiences, reducing linguistic and cultural barriers while fostering a supportive and inclusive learning environment. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 242 of 260 Background knowledge development is strengthened through targeted grammar instruction using resources such as the Sadlier Workshop and repeated exposure to academic vocabulary and writing conventions. Station rotations allow for differentiated support across varying English proficiency levels, ensuring all immigrant students can access instruction, build confidence, and develop foundational writing skills essential for academic success. Our Third Program, SEL Through the Arts, will serve approximately 20 students who will be divided into two groupings (approximately 10-15 immigrant students) in Grades K-2 afterschool, focusing on social-emotional development and language skills through artistic expression. This program will support language acquisition, build confidence, and enhance artistic abilities by providing a fun, supportive environment where students can boost self- expression. Students will learn art styles, build self-assurance, and strengthen connections with others through active participation and engaging texts. They will also expand their general English vocabulary by engaging in artistic projects, while collaborating to give peer-to-peer feedback. Throughout the program, students will be encouraged to take risks and challenge themselves. Instruction is delivered in Whole Group Format and facilitated by a Certified Bilingual Educator. The group is comprised of approximately 20 ELL's. Additionally, the program will support students in using their home language, offering assistance to students whose primary language is Spanish. In addition, paraprofessional support will be utilized, as needed, to support grouping dynamics. Instructional activities will include: -Whole-group stories to introduce SEL themes and vocabulary -Guided art projects paired with oral language prompts and reflection -Structured peer-to-peer sharing and feedback to promote expressive language and listening skills -Vocabulary development through discussion of emotions, art techniques, and creative choices The SEL Through the Arts program enhances instructional opportunities for immigrant students in Grades K–2 by combining social-emotional learning with language development through artistic expression. Creative activities provide a welcoming and accessible entry point for students who are new to U.S. schools, supporting confidence, self-expression, and engagement. Cultural adjustment is supported through whole-group stories and shared art experiences that introduce classroom routines, SEL concepts, and emotional vocabulary commonly used in U.S. schools. Instruction led by a Certified Bilingual Educator, along with home-language support for Spanish-speaking students, helps students feel culturally affirmed and comfortable participating. Background knowledge is developed as students build English vocabulary related to emotions, art techniques, and creative choices through guided projects and oral reflection. Structured peer sharing and feedback strengthen listening and speaking skills, while paraprofessional support ensures all students can meaningfully access instruction. The program will be supervised by Assistant Principals Ms. Jennifer Erichsen, Ms. Michelle Pinargote, and Ms. Martha Martinez. Supervisors working extended day programs work regular hours of 7:00 AM to 3:00 PM, 7:15 AM to 3:15 AM and 7:30 AM to 3:30 PM. After school programs will run from 2:21 to 4:21 PM. Before school programs run from 6:50 AM to 7:50 AM. Rationale for Title III Immigrant Funded Programs Describe the rationale for each Title III Immigrant funded program: For each program, include: DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 243 of 260 Qualitative and quantitative data analysis that support the need for program, and Data sources used to justify the need for the program Our First Program is designed to support newly arrived students to the country. This program is informed by data from our most recent EDUR and EDAT reports. According to the NYSESLAT 2025 results, newly arrived immigrant students in their first 1–2 years of enrollment demonstrated significantly lower proficiency in speaking, listening, reading, and writing compared to long-term ELL peers. Currently, 59.78% of our student population consists of English Language Learners (ELLs), many of whom come from immigrant families. Of this population, a substantial subset are newly arrived immigrant students who have had limited or interrupted formal schooling. Within this group, 15% are Entering ELL students who require additional English Language Acquisition in a supportive environment that is sensitive to their diverse needs. These students require targeted supplemental instruction beyond the core English Language Arts program due to limited English exposure, unfamiliarity with U.S. school structures, and gaps in academic language development. The data also highlights that our ELL and immigrant students need substantial support to achieve English language proficiency. Teacher observations and intake assessments indicate that newly arrived immigrant students struggle with oral language production, academic vocabulary, and classroom discourse. To address this, it is essential to provide resources for both school and home use, ensuring students have the tools they need to improve their language skills. Many of our newly arrived students have faced trauma and hardship and are in Temporary Housing, which must also be considered as we work to support their academic and emotional progress. Based on ATS housing data and EDAT reports, a significant percentage of newly arrived immigrant students are residing in temporary housing. This school year, approximately 35% of our student population were born outside the United States and require additional assistance in learning cultural norms and acquiring academic language to succeed. Our Second Program, Essential Writing Skills, was developed in response to data from our most recent EDAT report. EDAT literacy data and grade-level writing assessments indicate persistent writing gaps among immigrant students. Writing has been identified as a key area of need across most grade levels, particularly in Grades 1-5. It is important to note that ELLs have scored "low" in this modality, meaning they correctly answered fewer than 15% of the questions related to writing. This includes immigrant students who are developing foundational English writing skills while simultaneously acquiring academic language. This data highlights the critical need for explicit writing instruction to help students develop essential communication skills. Immigrant students require additional instructional time beyond the core literacy block to practice structured writing, oral rehearsal, and language development. Strengthening writing will not only support their English language development but also improve their performance on high-stakes testing. In addition, mClass data show that 54% of 1st graders and 42% of 2nd graders are well below benchmark in foundational literacy, inclusive of those of our immigrant population. To close these gaps and align with school and district literacy goals, a designated support in literacy connected with writing is necessary to support early reading proficiency and long-term academic achievement. Our Third Program, SEL Through the Arts (Grades K-2) was created to address the need for emotional support through artistic expression. According to our most recent EDAT report, 64.6% of our ELL population lives in temporary housing, and 65.7% did not demonstrate sufficient progress on the NYSESLAT examination. This data highlights the ongoing need for both emotional and academic support to help students make meaningful progress. Through this program, we aim to help students who have experienced trauma or face challenges related to their living situations build confidence, strengthen communication skills, and connect with peers who share similar experiences. We are also leveraging school survey feedback from families and staff, which identified SEL as a key area of focus. To enhance data-driven decision-making, we are implementing the 7 Mindsets SEL Assessment, a tool designed to measure students’ social-emotional learning competencies. This assessment provides valuable data to guide instruction and evaluate program effectiveness, offering a baseline for identifying student needs, tracking progress over time, and informing instructional adjustments that support student well-being, behavior, and academic growth. Collectively, these programs extend learning beyond the school day by providing targeted opportunities for DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 244 of 260 accelerated English language development through structured practice in speaking, listening, reading, and writing. They support students’ cultural adjustment and social-emotional well-being, which are critical for meaningful classroom engagement, while also building background knowledge and academic behaviors often assumed in core instruction. Through small group settings and bilingual support, the programs offer individualized scaffolding and additional instructional time that immigrant students need to fully access grade-level content and succeed academically. Students and Grade Levels to be Served For each Title III Immigrant funded program, describe the students that will be served: Include: Total number of students for each program, Grade levels for students in program, and If ELLs are also included in these programs, (e.g., Newcomer). We will offer afterschool programs designed to support our immigrant students, including Current ELLs, Special Education ELLs, Former ELLs, and students with IEPs in Grades K–5, across all levels of English language proficiency-Entering, Emerging, Transitioning, Expanding, and Commanding. The majority of participating students are Newcomer ELLs with 0–3 years of service. We remain committed to providing a diverse range of programs that address the unique academic and social-emotional needs of our immigrant families and their children. Our first afterschool program, designed for Newcomer ELL students (all in which are immigrant students), will serve approximately 20 students in Grades 2–5. Our second afterschool program, focused on Essential Writing Skills, will serve approximately 20 immigrant students in Grades 1–2, primarily targeting Newcomer ELLs. Our third afterschool program, SEL Through the Arts, will serve approximately 20 immigrant students (all proficiency levels) in Grades K–2, including members of our Newcomer ELL population. Grades K–5 Newcomer ELLs for all afterschool programs, including English Language Development (Grades 2–5), Essential Writing Skills (Grades 1–2), and SEL Through the Arts (Grades K–2), serving students across all English language proficiency levels. Rationale for Professional Development Describe the rationale for the professional development to be provided. For each activity, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis (e.g., patterns and trends identified) that support the need for the professional development, and Data sources (e.g., school survey teacher responses, in-house teacher surveys, teacher observation data) used to justify the professional development. For each activity include: DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 250 of 260 Qualitative and quantitative data analysis that support the need for the professional development Data sources used to justify the professional development According to the most recent English Language Learner Data Analysis Tool (EDAT) results, 65.7% of our ELL students have not yet met their annual progress targets. Many students remain below expected growth benchmarks, indicating a critical need to strengthen instructional strategies that accelerate language acquisition and academic achievement. A significant portion of our ELL population—82.8%—are identified as Newcomers, emphasizing the importance of targeted interventions designed for students who are newly arrived to the U.S. school system. Additionally, 54% of our ELL students are in Grades 1–3, a pivotal period for foundational literacy and language development. Findings from the NYC School Family Survey highlight additional areas for growth in school climate and student well-being. 55% of families reported feeling that the school adequately addresses safety and bullying. 68% of families indicated that their children are encouraged to share ideas and participate. These insights reflect the ongoing need to strengthen our approach to socio-emotional learning (SEL) and ensure that all students feel safe, valued, and engaged. As a result, professional learning will include targeted sessions on the Multi-Tiered System of Supports framework and strategies for fostering whole-child development—academically, socially, and emotionally. Additionally, staff will engage in professional learning focused on analyzing and integrating both academic data and SEL data (7 Mindsets) to inform instruction and provide more holistic support for our immigrant and ELL populations. The EDAT results indicate that writing is our greatest area of need, with additional support required in speaking, particularly in the foundational grades. These findings are consistent with teacher feedback collected through internal surveys and needs assessments, which highlight a strong demand for professional development focused on scaffolding strategies that help ELLs access and engage with academic content effectively. To address these needs, our professional learning plan will focus on equipping educators with effective tools and strategies to support language development across all content areas. A component of this plan includes unpacking the NYSESLAT to examine performance trends and identify instructional practices that foster student growth. Teachers will engage in professional learning grounded in Academic and Linguistic Demands: Creating Access to the Next Generation Learning Standards in English Language Arts for Linguistically Diverse Learners, aligned with the Literacy Shifts in K–5 and the use of the mCLASS platform to advance biliteracy development. Patterns in teacher observations suggest inconsistent implementation of intentionally scaffolded, rigorous writing tasks for multilingual learners, including opportunities for oral rehearsal prior to writing, and gradual release of support. Additionally, trends indicate a lack of explicit and interactive academic vocabulary instruction, including intentional word selection, multiple exposures across modalities, and structured opportunities for students to use academic language in speaking and writing. These professional learning opportunities (MTSS, Scaffolding Writing & Teaching Academic Vocabulary, NYSESLAT Unpacking) align directly with observation data, which highlight a need to strengthen targeted feedback on both content and language, and increase evidence of multilingual learners being actively engaged, increasingly independent, and accurately using academic vocabulary in written and oral responses in both small group and whole group instruction. Writing and vocabulary instruction supports both immigrant students and immigrant students who are ELLs because academic language is essential for success across all content areas. While ELLs may require additional language scaffolds, non-ELL immigrant students may also be developing academic vocabulary and writing expectations. Explicit instruction in writing and vocabulary helps all students access grade-level content, strengthen comprehension, and effectively communicate their understanding. With approximately 1/4 of our programming servicing Immigrant students who are Non-ELLs (15) these are essential skills to continue to develop academic competencies in reading and writing development. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 254 of 260 Part D: Family Engagement Activities Part D: Family Engagement Activities Family Engagement Summary Provide a summary of your school’s family engagement activities under your Title III Immigrant program. Describe the activities that families of immigrant students (including ELLs) will engage with by topic. Please make sure to include whether the activities will be funded by Title III Immigrant funds. Please note: If family engagement activities will be at funded by Title III Immigrant funds, vendor cost is calculated as part of OTPS and must not exceed 20% of the total allocation; teacher per-session is calculated as part of family engagement and must not exceed 10% of the total allocation. Elm Tree Elementary School currently serves a student population in which approximately 60% are identified as English Language Learners and approximately 35% of our student population are not native to the United States. This data reflects the rich linguistic and cultural diversity of our school community, with many students coming from immigrant families who are still developing English language proficiency. To support the academic and social success of our ELLs, it is essential to engage not only the students but also their families. Research and experience show that the most impactful plans for student growth involve families as active partners in the learning process. Families are invaluable stakeholders in their children’s academic achievement and social emotional well-being. Family engagement activities will be developed based on families’ expressed interests, as identified through Family Surveys distributed at the start of the year. More than 50% of the students are Multilingual Learners, indicating that many parents—particularly those from immigrant backgrounds—need additional support in helping their children develop English proficiency, especially in reading and writing. Furthermore, findings from both in-house surveys and the DOE School Survey reveal that many families would like more support around social-emotional learning (SEL) components, including safety, bullying prevention, positive classroom behavior, and fostering student participation and voice in the classroom. We know that family involvement is essential for the success of all students, especially our immigrant students. Many are acquiring a new language and need consistent and ongoing support in order to help their children. Therefore, we will engage parents by offering family workshops in which support and resources will be provided. All presentations will be translated into the home language of the families in attendance. Family workshops will include, but are not limited to (Note: Dates/Times are tentative and subject to change): Supporting Diverse Learners at Home in Building Language and Literacy Skills Reading Strategies for Families Understanding How to Read Family Reports from Assessment Systems (I-Ready and mClass) to better support learning for home use Understanding the NYSESLAT and how to Support Students at Home Supporting Families in the Summer Months to prepare their children for the Upcoming School Year The facilitation of these professional learning activities will be at no cost to the Title III Immigrant Plan By fostering strong school-family partnerships, we aim to create a collaborative environment that supports the academic and linguistic success of all ELL students and ones who are of immigrant families. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 255 of 260 In addition, we plan to partner with Generation Ready to provide an additional layer of support for our families— empowering them to be Leaders at Home with their children and addressing socio-emotional topics identified as priorities by our community. Rationale for Family Engagement Activities Describe the rationale for the family engagement activities to be provided: For each activity, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis (e.g., reasons for topics, time activities will be offered), and Data sources (e.g., parent survey responses, in-house parent surveys, PTA/PA request) used to justify the need for activities. Throughout the course of the program, we will maintain focused approaches that support Diverse Learners in developing and strengthening their language and literacy skills. In addition, we will provide ongoing family engagement activities designed to empower families as leaders within their homes and communities. These activities are informed by the following data: Recent EDUR data indicate that 59.78% of our student population are English Language Learners (ELLs). EDAT data show that 82.8% of our ELL population are Newcomers with 0–3 years of service. Recent mCLASS results show a 34% proficiency rate school-wide. EDAT data also reveal that 64.6% of our ELL and immigrant families live in Temporary Housing, an at-risk indicator for future academic success and a signal of increased need for family and parental support. We administer school-wide surveys three times a year—promoted through school communications and PTA meetings—to assess family needs. Additionally, we conduct individual surveys after each family engagement activity to inform next steps and identify necessary supports. Based on this feedback, most parents have indicated a need for language support and assistance with language development at home. In response, we have aligned our Family Engagement opportunities to address the specific needs of our immigrant families and provide targeted support moving forward. Schedule and Duration For each Title III Immigrant family engagement activity, include: Start and end dates/months, Total number of sessions, Days of sessions, and Time and duration of sessions. DBN: 24Q211 School: Elm Tree Elementary School 256 of 260 Monthly Family Workshops will begin on October 3, 2025, and will be held on either the second or third Tuesday of each month from 8:30 AM to 9:30 AM, following a rotating schedule. Workshops will continue through June 23, 2026, with a total of nine sessions offered during the school year. For families who prefer afternoon times, an additional session will be available on the same day from 2:45 PM to 3:45 PM. Workshop topics will be determined based on family needs and interests, as identified through an interest inventory survey distributed at the start of the year. This ensures that each session is relevant, responsive, and supportive of families’ goals for their children’s academic and language development. Tentative workshop dates: October 21, November 25, December 16, January 20, February 24, March 24, April 14, May 19, June 23 Title III Immigrant Family Engagement Activities will run from October 21, 2025, to June 23, 2026. These will consist of eight one-hour sessions on Tuesdays from 8:30 AM to 9:30 AM, with additional sessions added as needed based on family interest as the year progresses. Topics to be Covered For each Title III Immigrant family engagement activity, include topics to be covered. Supporting Diverse Learners at Home in Building Language and Literacy Skills: In these sessions families will be provided with engaging and FREE resources that can be used at home to assist families with English Language Development, as well as ways to best support their child’s educational and socio-emotional needs. We will also discuss the importance of bilingualism and strengthening their home language use at home. Reading Strategies for Families: In these sessions families will be provided with Reading Strategies and techniques to support literacy skills at home. The workshop will be conducted in English/Spanish, as well as translated in additional languages, as indicated by families prior to the workshop date. Understanding How to Read Family Reports from Assessment Systems (I-Ready and mClass): In these sessions, families will understand how to read the Family Reports from mClass and I-Ready in order to better support student learning at home. Understanding the New York State English as a Second Language Achievement Test: In this session, families will learn about the components of the NYSESLAT examination that is administered yearly to students who are identified as English Language Learners (ELL’s) and Multilingual Learners (ML’s). They will also be provided with resources to help students in preparation for taking the examination. Supporting Families with Summer Learning: In this session, we’ll explore creative strategies and activities that make learning enjoyable, from reading challenges and STEM projects to nature walks and learning games. They will leave with practical tools, resources, and tips to keep your child engaged and motivated, helping them retain knowledge and build new skills. The SEL Family Leadership and Empowerment Workshops: These sessions are designed to strengthen families’ ability to support their children’s social and emotional well-being while fostering leadership skills at home and within the community. Through interactive sessions, families explore strategies to build resilience, empathy, and effective communication. The workshop also empowers parents and caregivers to take an active role in their child’s learning journey, promoting collaboration between home and school for student success. Additionally, we will engage Ms. Aremni Francisco (Program Director for CBO) to provide further support. Family Notification of Activities and Translation and Interpretation Supports Describe: How families will be notified of Title III Immigrant family engagement activities (e.g., phone calls, emails), How notification of activities will be translated into parent/guardians’ preferred language, and How interpretation supports will be provided at events. Families will be notified of all school events and workshops throughout the year via our school website, which features built-in translation capabilities to ensure accessibility in each family's home language. This ensures that all families can stay informed and engaged, regardless of their primary language. In addition, our Parent Coordinator sends timely notifications through the Elm Tree App, which delivers updates in each family's preferred language. To further support communication, a comprehensive School-Wide Calendar is published on our website, providing families with an overview of all upcoming events and activities. When translation services are required for in-person events, we prioritize utilizing school-based staff who are fluent in the needed language. If additional support is necessary, we proactively coordinate with the Office of Language Services to ensure appropriate interpretation is available. Maintaining Records Describe how your school will maintain documentation (e.g. agendas, attendance sheets) of family engagement activities and notifications to families. All documentation related to family engagement activities and communications (inclusive of agendas and attendance sheets) is maintained by our Parent Coordinator, Ms. Gisela Catarine. She is supported in this effort by Assistant Principals Ms. Jennifer Erichsen, Ms. Michelle Pinargote, and Ms. Martha Martinez, as well as our ENL Coordinator, Ms. Nicole Gagliardi. Together, this team ensures that all outreach and engagement efforts are accurately recorded and aligned with school-wide goals for family involvement.
